❯ ok, for writing the paper, do you recommend going section by section and      
  having me check each one, or you produce an entire draft and we start         
  revising that?                                                                

⏺ I recommend going section by section, in a set order and with a short outline
  agreed first. Here's why:

  - You prefer to check each step. A full draft would put a lot of text in front
    of you at once, and early mistakes (a wrong framing, a number read the
    wrong way) would spread through every later section.
  - The sections depend on each other. The results decide what the discussion
    can say, and the discussion decides what the introduction and abstract
    promise. Writing them in order means each one rests on text you've already
    approved.
  - Time. The deadline is Monday, so we have about three days. Doing it section
    by section still fits if each round is quick.

  Order I'd suggest:
  1. Skeleton: the Quarto file with headings, the figure and table code that
     reads the saved data, and a one-line note of the point each section makes.
     You check that it renders and that the plan is right.
  2. Data: the source, what's measured, the cleaning steps, and the variables.
  3. Results: the figures, the tables and the numbers.
  4. Discussion: what the results mean, the weaknesses, and next steps.
  5. Introduction.
  6. Abstract and title last, because they summarise what's actually there.
  7. Final pass: banned words, cross-references and citations, then render to
     PDF.

  I'd also commit after each section you approve, so we can undo cleanly if
  needed.

  Want me to start on the skeleton? I'd read other/notes/ and the paper
  instructions first.

⏺ Edit scripts/05-exploratory_data_analysis.py:

  Dots. s is the marker area in points², so doubling s makes each dot about 1.4
  times wider.
  - Line 146: s=40 for the blue sites you keep. Try s=80.
  - Line 156: s=40 for the orange sites you left out. Change it to the same
    value as line 146.
  - Lines 149 and 159: linewidth=1 sets the white outline. Raise it to about 1.5
    if the outline looks thin on the bigger dots.

  Text
  - Line 173: fontsize=9 sets the beach names. Try 11 or 12.
  - Line 185: fontsize=8 sets the "60W (listed as …)" labels. Try 10.
  - Line 199: the legend has no size set, so it uses the default of 10. To
    change it, add fontsize=11 to axis.legend(...). You can also add
    markerscale=... if you want the legend dots to change size too.
  - Line 196: attribution_size=6 sets the Esri credit. You can usually leave it.

  Label positions. Bigger text and dots may overlap their neighbours. If they
  do, adjust the label offsets in points in LABEL_OFFSETS (lines 99–110). The
  labels to watch are the crowded Toronto Islands ones: Hanlan's Point,
  Gibraltar Point, Centre Island and Ward's Island. Line 182, xytext=(0, 12),
  sets the offset for the left-out site labels and may need to go up to about
  14.

  Then regenerate the map with uv run scripts/05-exploratory_data_analysis.py.

       89 +*Points: summary of the finding; possible reasons from geograp

          + River near Sunnyside, storm-water outfalls, island against ma
          +inland beaches). Hypotheses, not tested here.*                
       90  
       96 -## Model set-up                                               
       91 +## A warning describes yesterday's water {#sec-disc-warning}  
       92  
       98 -Define $y_i$ as the number of seconds that the plane remained 
          -aloft. Then $\beta_i$ is the wing width and $\gamma_i$ is the 
          -wing length, both measured in millimeters.                    
       93 +*Points: the one-day lag; water changes faster than the testin
          +g can follow; same-day methods such as qPCR (literature).*    
       94  
      100 -\begin{align}                                                 
      101 -y_i|\mu_i, \sigma &\sim \mbox{Normal}(\mu_i, \sigma) \\       
      102 -\mu_i &= \alpha + \beta_i + \gamma_i\\                        
      103 -\alpha &\sim \mbox{Normal}(0, 2.5) \\                         
      104 -\beta &\sim \mbox{Normal}(0, 2.5) \\                          
      105 -\gamma &\sim \mbox{Normal}(0, 2.5) \\                         
      106 -\sigma &\sim \mbox{Exponential}(1)                            
      107 -\end{align}

          +'s open data portal [@tphdata; @opendatatoronto]. The program 
          +is run jointly by Toronto Public Health, Toronto Water, the po
          +lice Marine Unit and the City's parks division, under a provin
          +cial protocol whose stated aim is to reduce water-borne illnes
          +s [@tphdata]. Every summer, from late May to Labour Day, staff
          + take a water sample each day at four to six fixed sites along
          + the shore of each of the city's ten supervised beaches (@fig-
          +sites-map). A laboratory counts the E. coli in each sample, an
          +d when the level at a beach is too high, Toronto Public Health
          + posts signs warning against swimming [@tphbeachwq].          
       40  
       41  ![Each of Toronto's ten swimming beaches is sampled at four to
            six sites along the shore. Blue points are the 50 sites this 
           paper uses. The two orange points, sites 60W and GP6, were add
           ed in 2026 and sit 14 to 16 km from the beaches they are liste
           d under, so they are left out. Map tiles from Esri.](figures/s
           ampling-sites-map.png){#fig-sites-map}
       42  
       43 +The copy used here was downloaded on 22 September 2026. It has
          + 102,534 rows, one for each site on each sampling day from 3 J
          +une 2007 to 16 September 2026, and each row gives the beach, a
          + site code, the date the sample was collected, the E. coli res
          +ult and the site's location. After leaving out rows with no re
          +sult, one implausible reading, two sites listed under the wron
          +g beach and dates outside the season (@sec-appendix-cleaning),
          + 98,370 results remain, from 50 sites on 2,019 days across 20 
          +years.                                                        
       44 +                                                              
       45 +E. coli is measured as a sign of faecal pollution rather than 
          +because it is the main danger itself. It lives in the gut of p
          +eople and other warm-blooded animals, so finding it in lake wa
          +ter means that sewage, storm-water runoff or bird droppings ha
          +ve reached the water recently, and with them the viruses and b
          +acteria that make swimmers ill [@sanchez2021]. Swimming in wat
          +er above the City's standard raises the risk of ear, eye, nose
          +, throat and skin infections, and swallowing it can cause stom
          +ach upset, vomiting and diarrhoea. Young children, older peopl
          +e and people with weakened immune systems are most at risk [@t
          +phabout].                                                     
       46 +                                                              
       47 +Toronto's standard is 100 E. coli per 100 mL of water. Ontario
          + and Health Canada use 200, the figure in the federal guidelin
          +es since 2012, while Toronto has kept the earlier and stricter
          + 100 [@tphabout; @sanchez2021]. This paper uses 100 throughout
          +, because it is the level at which Toronto posts warnings.    
       48 +                                                              
       49 +The data describe water, not people, and hold no personal info
          +rmation. What is at stake is the advice built on them. A warni
          +ng posted when the water is clean costs swimmers a beach day, 
          +a warning missed when the water is dirty exposes them to a hea
          +lth risk, and both happen under the current system [@saleem202
          +3]. The City's beach water quality page also offers each beach
          +'s daily swimming conditions for download [@tphbeachwq]. This 
          +paper uses the open data set instead because it gives every in
          +dividual result, so each day's status can be worked out the sa
          +me way for all twenty years.                                  
       50 +                                                              
       51 +The analysis uses Python [@python], with Polars [@polars] and 
          +NumPy [@numpy] for the data, statsmodels [@statsmodels] for th
          +e model, pointblank [@pointblank] to test the data, matplotlib
          + [@matplotlib] and contextily [@contextily] for the figures, a
          +nd Quarto [@quarto] for the paper.                            
       52 +                                                              
       53  ## Measurement {#sec-data-measurement}
       54  
       43 -*Points: from the lake to a row in the dataset. Sampling proto
          -col (depth, time of day, sites per beach), laboratory method, 
          -about 24 hours to a result. Reporting floor at 10 and, from 20
          -18, ceiling at 1,000: properties of the laboratory's reporting
          -, not of the water. Sunnyside has four sites, Kew Balmy six, t
          -he rest five.*                                                
       55 +A number in the dataset is the end of a chain that starts with
          + a person wading into the lake. At each site, staff fill a ste
          +rile bottle to 200 mL, plunging it to about 30 cm below the su
          +rface where people swim. The provincial protocol asks for at l
          +east five samples per beach, spread across the swimming area [
          +@phobeach]. Most Toronto beaches have five sites; Kew Balmy ha
          +s six and Sunnyside four. The bottles are kept cold and dark, 
          +and must reach a Public Health Ontario laboratory and be teste
          +d within a day of collection [@phobeach].                     
       56  
       45 -@fig-raw-results shows every published result.                
       57 +The laboratory uses membrane filtration [@phobeach]. The water
          + is passed through a filter with pores small enough to trap ba
          +cteria, and the filter is laid on a growth medium on which E. 
          +coli colonies can be told apart from other bacteria. Overnight
          +, each E. coli cell grows into a colony large enough to see; t
          +he colonies are counted and the count is scaled to E. coli per
          + 100 mL. The method detects more than 90% of E. coli strains, 
          +but cells stressed by the lake may not grow and other bacteria
          + can interfere, so a single result carries error [@phobeach]. 
          +The process takes about a day [@tphabout; @tphbeachwq], so a r
          +esult reaches the public a day after the water it describes. W
          +hen no sample can be taken, usually because of bad weather, th
          +e row is kept with its result left blank [@tphabout]; 3.5% of 
          +rows are blank.                                               
       58 +                                                              
       59 +The way results are reported leaves its mark on the numbers, a
          +s @fig-raw-results shows. Nearly all results (99.9%) are multi
          +ples of 10, and half (49%) are exactly 10. Ten is the lowest v
          +alue the laboratory reports, so a result of 10 means "10 or fe
          +wer": the data have a floor. From 2018 a second limit appears 
          +at the top. Results of exactly 1,000 become common while resul
          +ts above 1,000 become rare, and in 2026 none is above 1,000, a
          +lthough before 2018 readings reached 15,820. From 2018, then, 
          +a result of 1,000 is best read as "1,000 or more". Both limits
          + come from the reporting, not the water. The floor hides diffe
          +rences among the clean beaches, and the ceiling hides how bad 
          +the worst days were.

  What's in the Data section
  - 2.1 Source and context:
    - Who runs the sampling program and why.
    - What one row of the data is, and how many rows are left after cleaning.
    - Why E. coli is measured and what the health risks are.
    - Toronto's limit of 100 against the Ontario and federal 200.
    - Ethics: the data describe water, not people, but warning errors have
      costs.
    - An alternative data source.
    - The software used.
  - 2.2 Measurement: the path from a person wading in with a bottle to a row in
    the data: how samples are taken, how the lab counts E. coli, the one-day
    delay, blank rows, and the reporting floor of 10 and ceiling of 1,000.
  - 2.3 E. coli results by beach: a new table of summary statistics for each
    beach, plus the bar-chart figure. The table is built in the paper from
    analysis_data.csv, so the paper now runs Python. Render it with uv run 
    quarto render from paper/ so it uses the project's Python.
  - 2.4 Days over the limit:
    - The geometric mean in plain words, with a worked example: 10, 10, 10, 10
      and 1,000 average 208 but have a geometric mean of 25.
    - The rule the paper uses to call a day over the limit.
    - Why "over the limit" is not the same as "posted".
    - Why counting days instead of samples changes the shares only a little.
    - The one-to-three-sentence note on the ceiling hiding days over the limit.
  - References: eight sources from other/literature/ (the City and Public Health
    Ontario web pages, the Open Data Toronto dataset and portal, and the three
    journal articles) plus the software. I removed the starter-template entries.

  Things for you to check
  1. Possible better source for postings. The City's beach water quality page
     offers "Beach Condition Data for Researchers" as a JSON download. If it
     records the warnings that were actually posted, the paper could compare its
     computed days over the limit with real postings. Right now the paper only
     says it uses the per-sample results instead. I haven't looked at what the
     file contains. It could be worth a quick look after the paper is done, or
     it could go under next steps.
  2. Two claims to confirm. I cited Saleem et al. (2023) for Toronto posting
     decisions being based on a two-day geometric mean. I cited Sanchez et al.
     (2021) for E. coli as a sign of faecal pollution and for the 2012 federal
     limit of 200.

     figures each sit on their own page. I'd fix both in the final pass once all
     the text is in, because the page breaks will keep moving until then.

     convention, and I'd do it in the final pass as well.

❯ The data are the E. coli results from Toronto’s beach water sampling program, 
  published by                                                                  
  Toronto Public Health on the City’s open data portal (Toronto Public Health   
  2026; City of                                                                 
  Toronto 2026b).                                                               
                                                                                
  is this the proper way to cite the references? is another option brackets,    
  which to me would be more clearly a reference?  I'm used to numbers from my   
  chemistry papers                                                              

⏺ Yes, that format is correct. It's the author-date style, which is Quarto's
  default (Chicago author-date). Stats and social science papers usually cite
  this way. Your paper instructions allow any major style, so numbered citations
  are fine too.

  Your options:
  - Author-date (current): "(Sanchez et al. 2021)". The reader sees who and when
    without turning to the reference list. The cost is longer sentences,
    especially with two or three sources in one bracket.
  - Numbered: "[1]" or a superscript¹, with references listed in the order
    they're first cited. This is what you know from chemistry papers. You can
    get it by adding one style file (.csl) to paper/ and a csl: line to the
    header. For example:
    - american-chemical-society.csl gives superscripts, as in ACS journals.
    - ieee.csl gives [1] in brackets.
    - vancouver.csl gives [1] in brackets, as in medical journals.

    The citation code in the text doesn't change. Where a sentence names the
    authors, such as "Sanchez et al. (2021) found…", it becomes "Sanchez et al.
    [1] found…".

  My recommendation: use numbered brackets, with IEEE or Vancouver. You find
  them clearer, and they shorten the text, which helps with your worry about
  length. Many of the citations are City web pages, and "(City of Toronto
  2026a)" against "(City of Toronto 2026b)" tells the reader very little anyway.

  Should I switch to numbered? If so, say brackets or superscripts.

❯ Let's work on the title. The main title should be descriptive, like E. coli   
  levels at Toronto's beaches: Are some beaches more dangerous than others?     
  ⎿  1 skill available

⏺ Your structure (a descriptive phrase, then a question) is one of the formats
  in your class notes. One word I'd change is "dangerous". The paper measures
  how often the water is over Toronto's limit, not how many people get ill, so
  "dangerous" claims more than the data show. "Unsafe" fits better, because it's
  the City's own word for water above the limit.

  The rubric also asks for a subtitle that states the main finding, so here are
  three title-and-subtitle pairs.

  1. Recommended
     - Title: E. coli at Toronto's Beaches: Are Some Beaches Unsafe for Swimming
       More Often Than Others?
     - Subtitle: Two west-end beaches exceed the swimming limit on a third of
       summer days, eight times as often as the cleanest, and a warning based on
       the previous day's test misses most of those days
  2. Shorter subtitle, main finding only
     - Title: same as option 1
     - Subtitle: Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside exceed the swimming limit
       on a third of summer days, eight times as often as Gibraltar Point
  3. Plainer title
     - Title: E. coli Levels at Toronto's Beaches, 2007–2026
     - Subtitle: same as option 1

  My recommendation is option 1. The title asks the question, and the subtitle
  answers it and carries the paper's second finding. That fits your notes' point
  that the title, introduction and conclusion should all share the main
  conclusion. Option 2 is the one to pick if you'd rather keep the subtitle
  short and leave the warning finding to the abstract.

  Which do you want, or should I change something in one of them?

⏺ It comes from days, not samples. Your 35% and 6% are shares of single results
  (samples), from the table. The subtitle talks about days, and the day shares
  are lower, especially at Gibraltar Point:

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │         Beach          │ Days over the limit │ Ratio to Gibraltar Point │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Marie Curtis Park East │ 34.1%               │ 7.8                      │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Sunnyside              │ 31.8%               │ 7.2                      │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ Gibraltar Point        │ 4.4%                │ —                        │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  So "eight times" is right for Marie Curtis but overstates Sunnyside. By
  samples the ratios are 6.0 and 5.7, which is where your figure comes from.
  It's the same distinction the Data section makes between counting samples and
  counting days.

  A wording that is correct for both beaches:

  ▎ Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside exceed the swimming limit on a third of
  ▎ summer days, more than seven times as often as Gibraltar Point

  Shall I change the subtitle to that? The notes in
  other/notes/results_summary.md say "roughly eight times" in two places, and
  I'd correct those too so the error doesn't creep back into the abstract.

❯ your prose isn't very good. are you trying to write like an undergraduate?    

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ No, but I think I know what you're reacting to. Reading the Data section back,
  I wrote for a lay reader and made it too simple and too wordy. Specifically:

  - Lists, not arguments. Most paragraphs are strings of short facts with no
    opening sentence saying why they matter. The Measurement subsection walks
    through the sampling procedure step by step (bottle volume, depth, "kept
    cold and dark"), but the reader needs only the parts that affect the
    numbers.
  - Filler sentences: "A number in the dataset is the end of a chain that starts
    with a person wading into the lake", "The way results are reported leaves
    its mark on the numbers", "put another way", "then,".
  - Over-explaining. Defining the median and the log scale in full sentences
    reads as talking down. The instructions do ask for statistical terms to be
    explained, but a clause does that.
  - Too many numbers. 99.9%, 49%, 15,820, 3.5% and 22% all in a row, when only
    some of them support the point.
  - Loose terms. I switch between "result", "sample" and "reading" for the same
    thing.

  Here's the floor-and-ceiling paragraph rewritten, to check the direction
  before I redo the section.

  Before (8 sentences):

  ▎ The way results are reported leaves its mark on the numbers, as 
  ▎ @fig-raw-results shows. Nearly all results (99.9%) are multiples of 10, and 
  ▎ half (49%) are exactly 10. Ten is the lowest value the laboratory reports, 
  ▎ so a result of 10 means "10 or fewer": the data have a floor. From 2018 a 
  ▎ second limit appears at the top. …

  After (4 sentences):

  ▎ Two features of the laboratory's reporting shape every figure in this paper 
  ▎ (@fig-raw-results). No result is reported below 10, so the half of all 
  ▎ results that sit at 10 mean "10 or fewer". From 2018, high results are 
  ▎ increasingly reported as exactly 1,000; earlier ones reached 15,820, and in 
  ▎ 2026 none exceeds 1,000. The data are therefore censored at both ends, known
  ▎ only to lie below or above a value: the floor blurs differences among the 
  ▎ clean beaches, and the ceiling understates the worst days.

  If that's the register you want, I'll rewrite the whole Data section to match.
  I'd aim to cut it by about a third, give each paragraph one point stated at
  the start, and use "result" consistently. If something else is bothering you,
  point me to a passage and I'll start there.

❯ read the six pdf papers in course that start with numbers. those are six      
  papers that I wrote myself and were published. see if you can detect my style 
  from those papers                                                             

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Three of the papers are scans with no text layer, so I'll read those as page
  images. First, the three with text.

  Read 3 files, ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Two of the scanned papers wouldn't open through the PDF reader, so I'll turn
  their pages into images.

  Read 2 files, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I read all six. The three text papers (2007 BMCL, 2011 J. Med. Chem., 2023 ACS
  Med. Chem. Lett.) I read in depth; for the scanned ones (1996 and 1999 JACS,
  1999 Org. Lett.) I read the first page or all three pages. The style is
  consistent across 27 years.

  What your style does

  1. Goal, approach, result, explanation. Paragraphs open with what you were
     trying to do, then how, then what happened and why. "We set out to decrease
     the hERG activity by decreasing the basicity of the amine … while at the
     same time maintaining activity against resistant organisms." "We sought to
     maintain the favorable properties of 1: …"
  2. First person plural. "We undertook a campaign…", "we were pleased to see…",
     "To our delight, the Dess–Martin periodinane reacted quickly…" You use
     active voice for decisions and passive only for routine procedure.
  3. Numbers make the argument, usually as comparisons. "3-fold more potent than
     lead compound 1", "(10 nM vs 70 nM)", "lowering the clogP by 1.7 units",
     "(20 vs 18, 22 vs 21, and 27 vs 25)". A number almost always sits beside
     the thing it is compared with.
  4. Causal, measured interpretation. "likely due to poor absorption from low
     solubility, low permeability, or both", "consistent with the energy gain
     from…", "possibly from a desolvation penalty". You explain why, then hedge
     to the right degree.
  5. Long sentences with a clear shape. You use subordinate clauses and
     connectives (Because, Since, Thus, However, Furthermore, Finally) rather
     than strings of short statements.
  6. Background given briefly. Terms are defined once in parentheses: "mpk
     (milligrams of test compound per kilogram of animal weight)". Literature is
     covered in a few sentences: "…covered by Chen and Zhou up through 2019.
     Since then, …"
  7. Restraint. Almost no emphasis words. "Dramatic" appears once, and only
     where it's earned.
  8. A summary paragraph at the end. "In summary, we were able to optimize our
     previous lead 1 to discover 14c with improved…" It restates the arc of the
     paper with the key numbers.

  Where my draft departs from it: no "we", facts listed before the reader knows
  why, over-explained basics, numbers without comparisons, and choppy sentences.

  Here's the floor-and-ceiling paragraph redone in your style:

  ▎ The laboratory's reporting limits compress the data at both ends 
  ▎ (@fig-raw-results). Results below 10 are not reported, so the 49% of results
  ▎ at exactly 10 are best read as "10 or fewer". From 2018, high results were 
  ▎ increasingly reported as exactly 1,000; earlier results reached 15,820, 
  ▎ whereas none exceeded 1,000 in 2026, which suggests that a reporting ceiling
  ▎ was introduced. Because both limits narrow the range, differences among the
  ▎ cleanest beaches and the severity of the worst days are likely understated,
  ▎ although the effect on our main measure, days over the limit, is small 
  ▎ (@sec-data-days).

  One consequence is that the paper should use "we" rather than "this paper".
  That also suits statistics writing, including the course textbook.

  If this matches what you had in mind, I'll save it as the style guide for this
  paper and rewrite the Data section in it. I'd also cut it by about a third,
  per the concise-clear-correct rule.

⏺ I agree on cutting a figure, but I'd cut Figure 3, not Figure 4. Figures 2 and
  4 show different variables, and the paper needs both.

  - Figure 2 (every result, by year) shows the raw measurement: one point per
    sample. That's where the floor at 10, the ceiling at 1,000 from 2018 and the
    removed outlier are visible. It doesn't separate the beaches, though, so it
    can't show the paper's main finding.
  - Figure 4 (every beach-day, by beach) shows the variable the analysis
    actually uses: the daily geometric mean, which is what Toronto's limit
    applies to. It's the only figure that shows every observation behind the
    34%-against-4% comparison. Your notes (points_for_the_paper.md) name it as
    one of the two figures that carry the answer to the main question.
  - Figure 3 (bar chart of results by magnitude, by beach) is the one I'd drop.
    It summarises samples, not the days the analysis counts, and Table 1 already
    gives the same per-beach shares: at 10, above 100, median and 90th
    percentile. Its long caption exists mainly to explain why samples and days
    differ, which is extra work for the reader.

  Dropping Figure 3 would also let the "samples against days" paragraph shrink
  to a sentence, which helps with length.

  That would leave four figures (map, raw results, beach-days, model estimates)
  plus the summary table and the warning table.

✻ Baked for 10s · done 9:25 AM

❯ My suggestion is to change Figure 3 to showing the histograph of the          
  beach-days, not the individual measurements. Then Figure 3 is the main figure 
  answering the question from the title.                                        

⏺ I like that. A histogram of beach-days answers the title question in the
  paper's own unit, and it's easier to read than the dot plot in Figure 4. It
  also still counts every observation: the bars add up to all 19,662 beach-days,
  which is what the rubric means by showing the actual data. So Figure 3
  becomes the main figure, and Figure 4 goes.

  Design choices

  1. Bins.
     - Coarse: the same four bins as the current Figure 3 (10, 11–100,
       101–1,000, over 1,000). Very easy to read, but it hides the shape.
     - Fine: about 15–20 bins, evenly spaced on the log scale, with the dashed
       line at 100. This shows the whole distribution, for example how far past
       the limit Marie Curtis and Sunnyside go on their bad days.

     I'd go with fine bins and colour the bars above 100 orange. A labelled
     share per panel ("34% of days over the limit") gives the reader the
     headline number directly.
  2. Layout. Keep ten panels, one per beach, ordered from the largest share over
     the limit to the smallest, as now. That order makes the answer visible at
     a glance.
  3. The floor. 22% of beach-days have every sample at 10, so the lowest bin
     will be a tall spike, especially at the clean beaches. I'd label it in the
     caption rather than hide it, because it's real and Figure 2 already
     explains why it exists.

  What this changes elsewhere
  - The paragraph comparing samples with days mostly goes away. One sentence
    saying that the day shares are close to the sample shares in Table 1 is
    enough.
  - Table 1 stays at the sample level, so it complements the figure instead of
    repeating it.
  - The figure list becomes: map, all results (Figure 2), beach-day histogram
    (Figure 3), model estimates. Plus Table 1 and the warning table.

  Do you want fine or coarse bins? And what else about the structure do you want
  to discuss before I change anything?

⏺ The trial figure is drawn, and all 19,662 beach-days are included. It's saved
  in the scratchpad, not in the repo.

  What works
  - The two groups are clear at a glance. At Marie Curtis Park East and
    Sunnyside, the orange bars above 100 are comparable to the blue ones. At the
    other eight beaches, the ≤30 bin dominates and the orange bars are small.
  - At Sunnyside the 30–100 bin is taller than ≤30, and only there. Its typical
    day is higher, not just its bad days.
  - The labelled share gives the headline number in each panel.

  What needs fixing
  1. The x-axis labels collide ("300–1,0003,000"). I'd label the bin edges (10,
     30, 100, 300, 1,000, 3,000, 10,000) under the gaps between bars instead of
     naming each range. That fixes the collision and puts 100 exactly on the
     dashed line.
  2. The top two bins are almost invisible. 1,000–3,000 has at most 46 days per
     beach and 3,000–10,000 at most 14. Two options:
     - Merge them into "over 1,000", which breaks the half-log pattern at the
       end.
     - Keep them and print the count above small bars (e.g. "46", "14") so they
       don't read as zero. I'd do this, so the steps stay even.
  3. The lowest bin includes the 17 days below 10, so its range is really 1–30.
     Labelling bin edges makes that easy: the axis would start at 1 or the
     caption would say so.

  Shall I redraw it with bin-edge labels and counts on the small bars?

          -h the largest share above that limit to the smallest. Each bar
          - counts single samples rather than days: a beach is judged on 
          -the geometric mean of its four to six samples, and by that mea
          -sure Marie Curtis Park East Beach was over the limit on 657 of
          - its 1,925 sampled days (34%), against 35% of its samples.](fi
          -gures/results-by-magnitude.png){#fig-results-by-magnitude}    
      109 +![How often each beach went over the limit, 2007 to 2026. Each
          + bar counts the beach-days whose geometric mean falls in that 
          +range (19,662 days in total; days with fewer than four results
          + are left out). Orange bars, right of the dashed line, are day
          +s above Toronto's limit of 100 *E. coli* per 100 mL. The lowes
          +t bin includes the 17 days whose mean falls below the reportin
          +g floor of 10. Panels run from the largest share of days over 
          +the limit to the smallest.](figures/beach-day-histogram.png){#
          +fig-beach-days}                                               
      110  
      111  ## Days over the limit {#sec-data-days}
      112  
     ...
      114  
      115  In this paper a beach-day is over the limit when the geometric
            mean of that day's results is above 100. Only days with at le
           ast four results count, four rather than five so that Sunnysid
           e, with four sites, is included. That gives 19,662 beach-days,
            of which 2,816 (14%) are over the limit. @fig-beach-days show
           s every one. On 22% of beach-days every result was at the floo
           r of 10.
      116  
      117 -![Every beach-day, by beach. One point is the geometric mean o
          -f a beach's samples on one day, the figure the City's limit ap
          -plies to (19,662 days in total). Days with fewer than four res
          -ults are left out. Points are spread vertically so that they c
          -an be counted; the value itself is never moved. Orange points,
          - right of the dashed line, are the days above 100 *E. coli* pe
          -r 100 mL. Half of all samples are reported at 10, the lowest t
          -he laboratory measures, so the column at 10 is the days on whi
          -ch every sample was at that floor, and the stripes just above 
          -it are the values a geometric mean of four to six multiples of
          - ten can take. The 17 days whose mean falls below 10 are off t
          -he left of the axis.](figures/all-beach-days.png){#fig-beach-d
          -ays}                                                          
      118 -                                                              
      117  A day over the limit is not the same as a day the beach was po
           sted, for three reasons. The data do not record whether a warn
           ing went up. A study of the program describes posting decision
           s based on a geometric mean over two days rather than one [@sa
           leem2023]. And because results take a day, a warning posted to
           day reflects yesterday's water. "Over the limit" here means th
           at the water sampled that day exceeded 100, judged after the f
           act.
      118  
      119  Counting days rather than results changes the shares only a li
           ttle. At every beach the share of days over the limit is 1.2 t
           o 5.5 percentage points below the share of results above 100: 
           Marie Curtis Park East, for example, was over the limit on 34%
            of days and had 35% of results above 100. Averaging five resu
           lts might be expected to pull most days back under the limit, 
           but it does not, because a beach's sites tend to rise and fall
            together. When one is high, the others usually are too.

❯ OK, now to the Data section structure. What does the rubric and notes say we  
  need in that section (just a list please)                                     
⏺ From the task and checks:
  - The source of the data, and the broader context that produced it: ethical,
    statistical and otherwise.
  - Every variable of interest described and summarised with text, graphs and
    tables.
  - Graphs that show the actual data, or as close to it as possible, not summary
    statistics.
  - Graphs and tables cross-referenced in the text ("Table 1 shows…").
  - Data cleaning in an appendix, not in this section, unless something about it
    is essential.
  - A citation for the dataset and a separate one for Open Data Toronto.
  - No sections called "graphs" or "tables".

  From the rubric (Data):
  - A sense of the dataset.
  - The broader context of the dataset.
  - Every variable examined and explained.
  - Any similar datasets that could have been used, and why they weren't.
  - Any constructed variables mentioned.
  - High-level cleaning points of note mentioned, focusing on the result rather
    than the process.
  - Graphs, and possibly tables, of all observations, with discussion.
  - Summary statistics.
  - Relationships between the variables.
  - Too much detail can go to appendices.

  From the rubric (Measurement), as part of the Data section:
  - How a real-world event (the water) becomes an entry in the dataset.

  From your class notes (extra_instructions.md):
  - How the data are generated, from the City of Toronto.
  - A plot of all the data including the outlier, with the reason it was
    removed, perhaps in the caption.
  - Figure captions complete enough to understand without the text.
  - The data section can, and should, contain the descriptive analysis.

  From points_for_the_paper.md:
  - Samples compared with days: say once that the figures use different units.
  - "Over the limit" is not "closed" or "posted".
  - Toronto's limit of 100 compared with Ontario and Health Canada's 200, and
    which one the paper uses.
  - The reporting floor at 10 and the ceiling at 1,000 from 2018, as a short
    version.
  - One to three sentences on the ceiling possibly hiding days over the limit.
  - The two mislabelled sites: one sentence in the cleaning appendix, plus a
    footnote on the City's reply.

  Nothing is uncommitted.

  Your outline fits the rubric well. The story order works, and most gaps are a
  sentence or two that slot into sections you already have.

  Introduction
  - Fits: the context, the two questions and the section roadmap.
  - Missing:
    - The findings, with specific numbers. The rubric wants a reader to know
      from the introduction alone "what was done, why, and what was found".
    - The gap: nobody has compared the beaches over 20 years or checked the
      one-day delay.
    - Why it matters.

    Your class notes say the same: the introduction is a map of the whole paper,
    results included.

  Data
  - Fits:
    - Source and context.
    - The columns and how they're measured, including the floor at 10 and the
      ceiling at 1,000.
    - The constructed variable, the daily geometric mean.
    - The raw-results figure, with the removed point explained.
  - Missing:
    - Ethics, one or two sentences. The rubric names "ethical, statistical, and
      otherwise".
    - Similar datasets and why not, one sentence. This is already written.
    - Toronto's 100 against the province's 200, already written.
    - Summary statistics. Table 1 isn't in the outline, but the rubric asks for
      summary statistics explicitly.
    - The map (Figure 1). It isn't strictly required, but it's the only figure
      showing the site variable.
    - "Over the limit" is not "posted", one sentence, probably next to the
      geometric-mean definition.
  - One caution: the instructions say data cleaning belongs in an appendix
    unless something about it is essential. Your plan is fine if the Data
    section gives the two removals in a sentence each (the implausible reading
    and the two misplaced sites) and leaves the rest to the appendix. Your class
    notes suggest putting the outlier's explanation in the figure caption.

  Methods
  - Your "methods" bullet covers the geometric mean. The results also need a
    short explanation of two things before they appear:
    - The model behind the 95% intervals: what it compares (same-day differences
      between beaches), why days in the same month are treated as related, and
      what a 95% interval means in plain words.
    - How consecutive days are paired for the confusion matrix, and what a
      confusion matrix is.

    The rubric says every statistical concept must be explained. This could be a
    short "Methods" subsection at the end of the Data section rather than a
    separate Model section.

  Results
  - Fits: all three pieces.
  - Missing: one sentence showing the ranking isn't a few bad years (19 of 20
    years) or an effect of the ceiling (the split at 2018), and one sentence
    saying the reporting limits make the gaps between beaches look smaller than
    they are.

  Discussion
  - Fits: the summary, the reasons, and the improvements (weather model, qPCR).
  - Missing: weaknesses. Your class notes list them as a required part. The main
    ones:
    - Days are computed as over the limit, not recorded as posted.
    - The floor and ceiling understate the gaps.
    - There are no weather data.
    - Sunnyside has four sites.

  Not in the outline but required
  - Appendix: data cleaning. The instructions require cleaning to go there.
  - References.

  Optional appendices, which are already planned: the check on simulated data,
  and the table of pairwise comparisons.

  Do you want to update the outline yourself, or shall I add these as bullets in
  a different style so you can see what I added?

  Each figure and table is attached to the paragraph that first discusses it:
  Figures 1–4 and Tables 1–2.

  Some things changed along the way:

  - The "added" marking is gone. Once your points and mine were merged into
    paragraphs, it no longer separated anything, and you'd already approved the
    additions.
  - The Data section has three parts:
    - Source and context: three paragraphs, all already written.
    - Measurement: four paragraphs.

      the pairing of consecutive days, so every statistical idea is explained
      before it appears in the Results.

    19-of-20-years check is folded into the histogram paragraph, and the

❯ why aren't Figure 3 and Figure 4 in the Results section?                      

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It's LaTeX's float placement, not the order in the source. In paper.qmd, both
  figures sit inside the Results section. When LaTeX renders the PDF, though,
  figures are "floats": if a figure doesn't fit in the space left on the current
  page, LaTeX moves it to the top of a later page and keeps setting text.

  At the moment, Results and Discussion are only short placeholders, so both fit
  on page 7. Figure 3 is too tall for the space left there, so it floats to
  page 8 and Figure 4 to page 9. By then the Discussion has already been
  printed. The \newpage before the appendix then forces both figures out before
  Appendix A.

  Options

  1. Leave it for now. Once Results and Discussion have real text (probably 2–3
     pages), the figures will land much closer to their paragraphs. This was
     already on the list for the final layout pass.
  2. Force exact placement. Adding fig-pos: "H" to the paper's header puts every
     figure exactly where it appears in the source. The cost is gaps at the
     bottom of a page when a figure doesn't fit, which we'd tidy in the final
     pass.

  I'd go with option 2 now. You'll be reading the draft paragraph by paragraph,
  and it's easier when each figure sits next to its text. Shall I add it?

  to have them AFTER the Discussion header. it's okay to have lots of blank     
  space for now. Put all the figures and table right after the paragraph that   
  mentions them for now.

      51 -                                                               
      53  [Columns] A number in the dataset is the end of a chain that st
          arts with a person wading into the lake. At each site, staff fi
          ll a sterile bottle to 200 mL, plunging it to about 30 cm below
           the surface where people swim. The provincial protocol asks fo
          r at least five samples per beach, spread across the swimming a
          rea [@phobeach]. Most Toronto beaches have five sites; Kew Balm
          y has six and Sunnyside four. The bottles are kept cold and dar
          k, and must reach a Public Health Ontario laboratory and be tes
          ted within a day of collection [@phobeach].
      54  
      55  [Columns] The laboratory uses membrane filtration [@phobeach]. 
          The water is passed through a filter with pores small enough to
           trap bacteria, and the filter is laid on a growth medium on wh
          ich *E. coli* colonies can be told apart from other bacteria. O
          vernight, each *E. coli* cell grows into a colony large enough 
          to see; the colonies are counted and the count is scaled to *E.
           coli* per 100 mL. The method detects more than 90% of *E. coli
          * strains, but cells stressed by the lake may not grow and othe
          r bacteria can interfere, so a single result carries error [@ph
          obeach]. The process takes about a day [@tphabout; @tphbeachwq]
          , so a result reaches the public a day after the water it descr
          ibes. When no sample can be taken, usually because of bad weath
          er, the row is kept with its result left blank [@tphabout]; 3.5
          % of rows are blank.
      56  
      57  [Floor and ceiling] The way results are reported leaves its mar
          k on the numbers, as @fig-raw-results shows. Nearly all results
           (99.9%) are multiples of 10, and half (49%) are exactly 10. Te
          n is the lowest value the laboratory reports, so a result of 10
           means "10 or fewer": the data have a floor. From 2018 a second
           limit appears at the top. Results of exactly 1,000 become comm
          on while results above 1,000 become rare, and in 2026 none is a
          bove 1,000, although before 2018 readings reached 15,820. From 
          2018, then, a result of 1,000 is best read as "1,000 or more". 
          Both limits come from the reporting, not the water. The floor h
          ides differences among the clean beaches, and the ceiling hides
           how bad the worst days were.
      58  
      58 -[Floor and ceiling] The ceiling matters more once results are a
         -veraged. Since 2018, 34 beach-days include a result of exactly 
         -1,000 yet have a geometric mean below 100. On about 8 of them a
         - true reading of 2,000 or less would have put the day over the 
         -limit, so the count of days over the limit from 2018 on is, if 
         -anything, slightly low.

           reported in multiples of 10, which is why the low readings fal
          l into separate rows. The laboratory reports no results as zero

❯ OK, now rewrite these in my style:                                            
  [Swimming limit] Toronto’s standard is 100 E. coli per 100 mL of water;       
  Ontario and Health                                                            
  Canada use 200 [1,2]. This paper uses 100, the level at which Toronto posts   
  warnings.                                                                     
  [Ethics and alternatives] The data describe water, not people, and contain no 
  personal in-                                                                  
  formation. The stakes lie in the advice built on them: a needless warning     
  costs swimmers a                                                              
  beach day, and a missed one exposes them to illness [6]. The City also offers 
  daily swimming                                                                
  conditions for download [4], but this paper uses the open data because they   
  give every result,                                                            
  so each day’s status can be worked out the same way for all twenty years.     

⏺ Here are proposed versions. I haven't applied them.

  [Swimming limit]

  ▎ Toronto's standard of 100 E. coli per 100 mL is half that of Ontario and 
  ▎ Health Canada: Health Canada raised its guideline from 100 to 200 in 2012, 
  ▎ and Ontario follows it, whereas Toronto retained the earlier, stricter value
  ▎ [1,2]. Because 100 is the level at which Toronto posts warnings, it is the 
  ▎ limit used throughout this paper.

  - The comparison now comes first ("half that of"), and one clause explains why
    the two standards differ. That comes from Sanchez et al. [2]: the federal
    guideline was 100 before 2012.
  - The second sentence gives the reason before the decision.

  [Ethics and alternatives]

  ▎ Because the data describe water rather than people, they raise no privacy 
  ▎ concerns; the ethical stakes lie instead in the advice built on them, since 
  ▎ a needless warning costs swimmers a beach day and a missed one exposes them 
  ▎ to illness [6]. The City also offers each beach's daily swimming condition 
  ▎ for download [4], but the open data were used here because they give every 
  ▎ individual result, allowing each day's status to be calculated the same way 
  ▎ for all twenty years.

  - Three sentences become two, joined with "because", "instead" and "since" in
    place of separate short statements.
  - "were used here" is passive, as you asked, rather than "this paper uses".

  Apply both, or adjust first?

  ▎ Each row of the dataset records one site on one day: the beach, a site code

  ▎ result. To obtain each result, staff collect 200 mL of water about 30 cm 
  ▎ below the surface at each of the beach's sites (five at most beaches, six at
  ▎ Kew Balmy and four at Sunnyside), and a Public Health Ontario laboratory 
  ▎ counts the E. coli by membrane filtration, in which bacteria trapped on a 
  ▎ filter grow overnight into visible colonies that are counted and scaled to a
  ▎ volume of 100 mL [@phobeach]. Because the process takes about a day, a 
  ▎ posted result describes the previous day's water [@tphabout; @tphbeachwq]. 
  ▎ When no sample could be taken, usually because of bad weather, the result 
  ▎ was left blank [@tphabout]; 3.5% of rows are blank.

❯ yes, do [floor and ceiling] now                                               
⏺ Here's the proposed [Floor and ceiling] paragraph, merging the current two. I
  haven't applied it.

  ▎ The laboratory's reporting limits compress the data at both ends 
  ▎ (@fig-raw-results). Results below 10 are not reported, so the 49% of results
  ▎ at exactly 10 are best read as "10 or fewer". From 2018, high results were 
  ▎ increasingly reported as exactly 1,000; earlier results reached 15,820, 
  ▎ whereas none exceeded 1,000 in 2026, which suggests that a reporting ceiling
  ▎ was introduced. Because both limits narrow the range, differences among the
  ▎ cleanest beaches and the severity of the worst days are likely understated.
  ▎ The ceiling can also hide a day over the limit: since 2018, 34 days at a 
  ▎ beach included a result of 1,000 yet had a daily geometric mean 
  ▎ (@sec-data-methods) below 100, and on about 8 of them a true result of 2,000
  ▎ or less would have put the day over, so the number of days over the limit 
  ▎ since 2018 is, if anything, slightly low.

  What changed:
  - Opening: it now states the point, that the limits compress the data at both
    ends, instead of "leaves its mark on the numbers".
  - Cut: the 99.9%-multiples-of-10 detail, which the Figure 2 caption already
    covers.
  - Grammar: the ceiling evidence is now one sentence with a hedge ("suggests
    that").
  - The hidden-days point is kept, since your notes asked for one to three
    sentences on it. It now says "days at a beach" and "daily geometric mean"
    with a reference to Methods, because "beach-day" isn't defined until there.
  - Length: about 150 words, down from about 190.

  Apply it?

❯ please check over my edits to make sure they make sense and read well.        

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I compared your version of the [Floor and ceiling] paragraph with the one I
  applied. You made two edits, and both make sense. Each could read a little
  more smoothly.

  1. "Results below 10 are usually not reported"

  Adding "usually" is correct, because 63 results below 10 do exist. But
  "usually not" reads a little loosely. "Rarely" is tighter and says the same
  thing:

  ▎ Results below 10 are rarely reported, so the 49% of results at exactly 10 
  ▎ are best read as "10 or fewer".

  2. The new last sentence

  ▎ As a result, the number of days over the limit since 2018 may be slightly 
  ▎ higher than the one calculated from these data.

  Splitting it off as its own sentence helps, and "may be slightly higher" is
  clearer than my "if anything, slightly low". The weak spot is "the one
  calculated": "one" has to reach back to "number", and the sentence says
  "number" when it means the true count. A suggestion:

  ▎ The true number of days over the limit since 2018 may therefore be slightly 
  ▎ higher than the number calculated from these data.

  "Therefore" also keeps the causal link, where "As a result" repeats "result",
  which has just been used for lab results in the same paragraph.

  Everything else in the paragraph is unchanged from what you approved and reads
  well. Shall I apply either suggestion?

      53  [Columns] Each row of the dataset records one site on one day:

          lt, staff collect 200 mL of water about 30 cm below the surface

           Balmy and four at Sunnyside), and a Public Health Ontario labo
          ratory counts the *E. coli* by membrane filtration, in which ba
          cteria trapped on a filter grow overnight into visible colonies
           that are counted and scaled to a volume of 100 mL [@phobeach].
           Because the process takes about a day, a posted result describ
          es the previous day's water [@tphabout; @tphbeachwq]. When no s

✻ Churned for 21s · done 10:52 AM

❯ what python package are we using for tables?                                  

⏺ None. The two tables are built by hand: a short Python chunk uses Polars to
  compute the numbers, then prints a Markdown table row by row. Quarto (via
  Pandoc) turns that Markdown into the LaTeX table in the PDF. So the "table
  package" is really Quarto itself, which is already cited, and there's nothing
  extra to cite.

  The course post allows any graph or table package, so that's fine. If you'd
  like a dedicated package, the Python counterpart to R's gt and tinytable is
  great_tables (from Posit). It gives more control over formatting: column
  spanners, number formats, footnotes and styling. The trade-offs:
  - It's one more dependency to add and cite.
  - Its PDF output is less mature than its HTML output, so it may need some
    trial and error in Quarto.

  Our two tables are simple, and they already have thousands separators and
  sensible decimals, which is what the rubric asks for. I'd keep them as they
  are unless you want a specific formatting feature.

  strip both, so Quarto can add the numbered caption and the tables stay in
  place like the others.

      112 +def latex_tabular(table: GT) -> str:                          
      113 +    """The tabular from great_tables' LaTeX, without its float
          +ing wrapper and font size,                                    
      114 +    so that Quarto supplies the numbered caption and the table
          + stays in place."""                                           
      115 +    tex = table.as_latex()                                    
      116 +    start = tex.index("\\begin{tabular*}")                    
      117 +    end = tex.index("\\end{tabular*}") + len("\\end{tabular*}"
          +)                                                             
      118 +    return "```{=latex}\n{\\small\n" + tex[start:end] + "\n}\n
          +```"

⏺ The great_tables versions are in paper/paper.pdf: the Table 1 version is on
  page 5, and the Table 2 version (numbered Table 4) is on page 9. They show why
  I'd stay with the current tables:

  1. Column headings in the wrong place. In the great_tables version of Table 1,
     the "E. coli per 100 mL" heading sits over Sites and Results instead of
     Median and 90th percentile. "Share of results" is also shifted two columns
     left. I reproduced this with a six-column test: in great_tables 0.24's
     LaTeX output, a spanner that doesn't start at column 2 is drawn at column 2
     anyway. It's a bug in the library, not in our code.
  2. The row label column is broken. In the warning table, the "Yesterday's
     advice" heading is missing, and a stray vertical rule appears beside
     "Warning" and "All-clear".
  3. The tables float. Both drifted out of place: the Table 1 version prints
     above the original, and the Table 2 version landed after part of the
     Discussion. fig-pos: "H" only pins figures, so this part could be fixed
     with tbl-pos: "H". Points 1 and 2 couldn't be fixed without patching the
     library.
  4. No italics for E. coli in the heading.

  What great_tables does well is the grouped column headings and the cleaner
  stub column, but only its HTML output gets them right. For a PDF, the
  hand-built Markdown tables are simpler and correct.

  I'd remove both trial tables and take great-tables back out of pyproject.toml.
  uv add listed it there even though it was already installed through
  pointblank. Shall I do that?

⏺ Committed as 0517b86.

  Here's the proposed [Beach-days] paragraph, merging the current three. I
  haven't applied it.

  ▎ Because Toronto's limit applies to the geometric mean of a beach's results 
  ▎ on a day rather than to any single result [@young2023], the analysis works 
  ▎ with beach-days. The geometric mean of $n$ results is the $n$th root of 
  ▎ their product, or equivalently the average of their logarithms converted 
  ▎ back; it suits counts that span orders of magnitude because a single extreme
  ▎ result does not dominate it, so that results of 10, 10, 10, 10 and 1,000 
  ▎ give a geometric mean of 25 against an ordinary average of 208. A beach-day 
  ▎ was counted only when it had at least four results (four rather than five, 
  ▎ so that Sunnyside, with four sites, is included), giving 19,662 beach-days, 
  ▎ of which 2,816 (14%) were over the limit. A day over the limit is not 
  ▎ necessarily a day on which a warning was posted: the data do not record 
  ▎ postings, posting decisions may be based on a geometric mean over two days 
  ▎ [@saleem2023], and because results take a day, a warning posted today 
  ▎ reflects yesterday's water.

  What changed:
  - Opening: it leads with why the unit is beach-days, then defines the
    geometric mean. That definition now cites Young et al. [@young2023], which
    states that Toronto uses the geometric mean of the day's samples. It's in
    the bibliography but wasn't cited anywhere yet.
  - The three caveats about postings are now one sentence.
  - Removed:
    - "Figure 3 shows every one", since Figure 3 now sits in Results and this is
      Methods.
    - "On 22% of beach-days every result was at the floor", a detail that isn't
      needed here.
  - Length: 4 sentences, about 170 words, down from about 240.

  The [Not in outline] paragraph after this one compares day shares with sample
  shares. I'd delete it. Table 1 gives the sample shares and Figure 3 the day
  shares, and the two are close enough that a reader won't think they conflict.
  If you want to keep the point, it could shrink to a clause in the [Beach
  comparison] results paragraph.

  Apply the [Beach-days] paragraph, and delete [Not in outline]?

❯ look over my edits                                                            

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Your edits read well. Three small points, all in the last three sentences:

  1. The "--" dash. Pandoc turns "--" into an en dash (–), so the PDF shows
  "posted – the data…". That's acceptable, but the rest of the paper uses colons
  and semicolons for this kind of link, and a colon fits here because what
  follows explains the first clause:

  ▎ A day over the limit is not necessarily a day on which a warning was posted:
  ▎ the data do not record postings.

  2. "Saleem et al. report that posting decisions may be based…" Pairing
  "report" with "may be" hedges twice: they report it as fact, and the "may"
  doubts it. One or the other is enough:

  ▎ Saleem et al. describe posting decisions based on a geometric mean over two 
  ▎ days [@saleem2023].

  or, keeping your hedge without the attribution:

  ▎ Posting decisions may also be based on a geometric mean over two days 
  ▎ [@saleem2023].

  I slightly prefer the first. Naming the source shows it's their observation,
  not the City's stated rule.

  3. "In addition, a warning posted on one day reflects the previous day's 
  water." With "because results take a day" removed, this now repeats the
  [Columns] paragraph ("a posted result describes the previous day's water")
  without adding anything. It still belongs in the list of reasons, so I'd
  shorten it and point back:

  ▎ In addition, a posted warning reflects the previous day's water 
  ▎ (@sec-data-measurement).

  The earlier sentences you edited (the tighter geometric-mean definition,
  "Sunnyside (four sites)", and "were" in place of "are") all read well as they
  are.

  Shall I apply the three changes, using the first option in point 2?

✻ Sautéed for 44s · done 11:20 AM

❯ what's the canadian style for em-dash? with or without spaces?                

⏺ It depends which Canadian guide you follow:

  - The Canadian Style, the federal government's guide (Translation Bureau, now
    on the Language Portal of Canada), uses no spaces: "posted—the data".
  - The Canadian Press Stylebook, used by most Canadian newspapers, puts a space
    on each side: "posted — the data". That matches AP style in the US.

  For an academic paper, unspaced is the more common choice, and it's also what
  Chicago uses, which many Canadian journals follow. Your current spaced version
  is correct newspaper style, though, and would suit the rubric's "Financial
  Times reader". The FT itself, being British, uses a spaced en dash instead.

  The one thing that matters is consistency. This is currently the only dash in
  the paper, so either choice is fine. Do you want to switch to unspaced, or
  keep it?

⏺ Here's a proposed draft for [Model], based on the model code in
  src/toronto_beaches_ecoli/analysis.py and your notes in method_validation.md.
  It's long enough that I've split it into two paragraphs: the model itself, and
  how its uncertainty is handled. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Model] To test whether the differences reflect the beaches rather than the 
  ▎ weather on the days each was sampled, each beach was compared only with the 
  ▎ other beaches sampled on the same day. For beach $b$ on day $d$, the model 
  ▎ is
  ▎
  ▎ $$\log_{10} G_{bd} - \bar{L}d = \beta_b + \varepsilon{bd},$$
  ▎
  ▎ where $G_{bd}$ is the beach's geometric mean that day, $\bar{L}d$ is the 
  ▎ average of $\log{10} G$ over all beaches sampled that day, $\beta_b$ is the 
  ▎ beach's average gap from the others, and $\varepsilon_{bd}$ is the 
  ▎ day-to-day variation the model does not explain. Subtracting each day's 
  ▎ average removes anything that affects all beaches at once, such as a 
  ▎ city-wide storm. Because the model works on a log scale, a gap of $\beta_b =
  ▎ 0.3$ means about twice the E. coli of the average beach on the same day 
  ▎ ($10^{0.3} \approx 2$). The model was fitted by least squares using 
  ▎ statsmodels [@statsmodels].
  ▎
  ▎ [Model] Each estimate is reported with a 95% interval: if the analysis were 
  ▎ repeated over many different runs of summers, about 95% of such intervals 
  ▎ would contain the true gap. Because a wet week raises a beach's levels for 
  ▎ several days running, days in the same calendar month were treated as 
  ▎ related rather than independent, which roughly doubles the width of the 
  ▎ intervals. Two beaches were judged to differ when a gap as large as the one 
  ▎ observed would arise less than 5% of the time if they were in fact alike. 
  ▎ Testing all 45 pairs this way would produce about two false differences by 
  ▎ chance, so the comparisons were corrected by Holm's method, which raises the
  ▎ bar each comparison must clear so that the chance of any false difference 
  ▎ stays near 5%.

  Notes:
  - The equation. It's written the way the code actually fits the model: each
    day's average subtracted, then one gap per beach. Every symbol is defined in
    words, as the rubric requires.
  - Statistics explained in plain words: the log scale (with the 10^0.3 ≈ 2
    example), the 95% interval, the 5% threshold (the idea behind a p-value,
    without using the term), grouping by month, and Holm's correction.
  - Moved to Results: the "gaps shrink to about 65% of their true size" point,
    as in your outline.
  - Left out: the degrees-of-freedom caveat from your notes. Its effect is
    small, and it would need too much explanation for this reader.
  - Length: about 270 words, the longest section so far. If you'd rather keep
    [Model] as one paragraph, the easiest cut is the 5%-threshold sentence,
    since "correct for 45 comparisons" still reads without it. Or the second
    paragraph could become its own tag, such as [Uncertainty], added to the
    outline too.

  Apply it as two paragraphs?

  Read 2 files, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes. R² is 0.21. The notebook is at other/explore/model_explained.ipynb,
  already run, so open it in PyCharm or Jupyter to see the outputs.

  The key point: there's no line being fitted. The model has one term per beach
  and nothing else, and in that case least squares simply gives each β_b as the 
  average of that beach's day-relative values. The notebook checks this: the
  model's β and the plain mean agree exactly for all ten beaches. What the model
  adds over a plain average is the standard error and the 95% interval, with
  days in the same month treated as related.

  R², in context:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬──────┐
  │        What explains the raw log₁₀ daily geometric means         │  R²  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ The day alone (weather and anything else shared across the city) │ 0.32 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ The day and the beach together                                   │ 0.46 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼──────┤
  │ The beach, after each day's average is removed (our model)       │ 0.21 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴──────┘

  So which beach it is explains about a fifth of the variation left after
  removing each day's average. The rest is single-day noise at individual
  beaches, which is the ε in Equation 1. A modest R² doesn't weaken the finding.
  The question is whether the average gaps are real, and with about 2,000 days
  per beach those averages are pinned down tightly. A note on the software:
  without an intercept, statsmodels reports a non-standard ("uncentred") R², but
  here the two versions are identical because the outcome averages exactly
  zero.

  The plots:
  1. Ten histograms, one per beach. Each shows every beach-day as its gap from
     that day's average, with β as a red line and its 95% interval as a red
     band. Marie Curtis and Sunnyside are shifted to the right. The band is very
     narrow compared with the spread of single days, which is the "precise
     average, noisy days" point.
  2. Marie Curtis and Gibraltar Point over time. Every beach-day from 2007 to
     2026, with each beach's β as a flat line. The fitted "model" is just those
     two lines, and Marie Curtis sits above Gibraltar Point in every year.

  A suggestion for the paper: "The model was fitted by least squares" is
  correct, but it may suggest a more elaborate fit than there is. It would be
  clearer to say "…so each $\beta_b$ is simply the beach's average gap from the
  others, and least squares gives exactly that average". R² could also go in as
  one clause if you want it.

  The notebook isn't committed yet.

✻ Cooked for 1m 33s · done 11:39 AM

❯ since it's not fitting a line, I don't think R2 adds much. is there a way to  
  get the standard error and 95% CI without "fitting by least squares"?         

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes. Since each β is just an average, its standard error can be worked out
  directly, without mentioning least squares. I checked this against the model,
  and the numbers match.

  By hand, for one beach:
  1. β_b is the average of the beach's day-relative values, as before.
  2. Standard error. For each calendar month (96 of them), subtract β_b from
     each of that month's values and add them up, giving one total per month,
     S_m. Then
     SE = √(Σ S_m²) / n,
     where n is the beach's number of days. Summing within a month first is what
     "treating days in the same month as related" means: a run of high days
     counts as one lump of evidence, not many independent pieces.
  3. 95% interval: β_b ± 1.96 × SE.

  For all ten beaches, this hand calculation matches the model's standard errors
  to within 1%. For example, Marie Curtis Park East gives 0.0185 by hand and
  0.0186 from the model. The small difference is a routine small-sample
  adjustment in the software.

  A simpler-looking version I'd avoid: averaging each month first, then taking
  the SD of the 96 monthly averages divided by √96. It gives noticeably
  different numbers, for instance 0.0233 for Marie Curtis, because it counts a
  partial month of 8 days the same as a full month of 31.

  For the paper, the Methods text could drop least squares:

  ▎ Each $\beta_b$ is therefore the beach's average gap from the other beaches 
  ▎ sampled the same day. Its standard error was calculated from the variation 
  ▎ between calendar months rather than between days, because a wet week raises 
  ▎ a beach's levels for several days running.

  The code doesn't need to change, since it produces these same numbers, and
  statsmodels can stay cited because it computes the pairwise tests. I'd also
  leave R² out of the paper, as you suggest.

  Shall I add the hand calculation to the notebook as a check, and apply the new
  wording to the [Model] paragraphs?

⏺ Committed as ab9abbe.

  Here's the proposed [Consecutive days] paragraph. It explains the method and
  leaves the numbers to Results, apart from the pair count. I haven't applied
  it.

  ▎ [Consecutive days] To measure how well a posting based on the previous day's
  ▎ samples describes the water on the day it is read, each beach-day was 
  ▎ paired with the same beach's result on the day before. Days either side of a
  ▎ gap in sampling were not paired, which leaves 18,998 pairs. Treating 
  ▎ yesterday's result as today's advice gives four outcomes: a warning when 
  ▎ today's water is over the limit or under it, and an all-clear when today's 
  ▎ water is over or under it. The counts of these outcomes form a two-by-two 
  ▎ table, often called a confusion matrix. Two ratios summarise the table from 
  ▎ a swimmer's point of view: the share of days over the limit that a warning 
  ▎ caught, and the share of warnings that were needed. Overall accuracy, the 
  ▎ share of days on which the advice was right, is not used, because most days 
  ▎ are under the limit and a rule that never posted a warning would score 86%.

  Notes:
  - Goal first: it opens with the purpose, then the method.
  - "Confusion matrix" is named and explained in plain words, since the rubric
    wants every statistical term explained.
  - Accuracy: your points_for_the_paper.md says to leave it out. This paragraph
    gives the reason in one clause, so a reader who knows the term doesn't
    wonder where it went. The 86% is the share of days under the limit (16,296
    of 18,998).
  - Left out: the day-to-day correlation (0.42), which isn't in the outline.
  - Length: 6 sentences, about 150 words.

  Apply it?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The problem is that the current paragraph switches between "the day it is
  read", "today", "yesterday" and "the day before" without fixing which day is
  which. Here's a rewrite that anchors everything to the posting day, the day a
  swimmer sees the sign, whose advice comes from the previous day's samples. I
  haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Consecutive days] Because results take about a day, the advice on display 
  ▎ at a beach on any day, called here the posting day, is based on the samples 
  ▎ taken the day before. To measure how often that advice was right, each 
  ▎ posting day was paired with the previous day at the same beach, using only 
  ▎ days that were consecutive (18,998 pairs). The advice on the posting day was
  ▎ taken to be a warning if the previous day's geometric mean was over the 
  ▎ limit, and an all-clear if it was not. That advice was then compared with 
  ▎ the water sampled on the posting day itself, which gives four outcomes: a 
  ▎ needed warning (the water was over the limit), an unneeded warning (under), 
  ▎ a correct all-clear (under) and a missed warning (over). The counts of these
  ▎ four outcomes form a two-by-two table, often called a confusion matrix. Two
  ▎ ratios summarise it from a swimmer's point of view: the share of days over 
  ▎ the limit on which a warning was posted, and the share of warnings that were
  ▎ needed. Overall accuracy is not used, because most days are under the 
  ▎ limit, so a rule that never posted a warning would still be right on 86% of 
  ▎ days.

  What's clearer:
  - One reference day. The posting day is defined once. After that, "previous
    day" always means the sample behind the advice, and "posting day" always
    means the water the swimmer meets.
  - Each outcome is named with the water condition that goes with it.
  - "Today" and "yesterday" are gone from the paragraph.

  Knock-on change to Table 2: its caption and headings still use "Yesterday's
  advice" and "Today over the limit". To match, I'd change:
  - the row heading to "Advice posted",
  - the columns to "Water over the limit on the posting day" and "Under the 
    limit",
  - the caption to "…advice posted, from the previous day's samples, against the
    water on the posting day…".

  Apply the paragraph and the Table 2 wording?

✻ Cogitated for 8s · done 12:01 PM

❯ instead of "unneeded warning" isn't "false warning" better?                   

⏺ Yes, "false warning" is better. It's the plain-language form of the
  statistical term "false positive", and it pairs naturally with "correct
  warning". "Unneeded" was softer, but less precise.

  That raises one more choice, for the fourth outcome:

  - correct warning, false warning, correct all-clear, missed warning. "Missed
    warning" says what the swimmer lost: a warning that should have been posted.
  - correct warning, false warning, correct all-clear, false all-clear. Fully
    symmetric, and it matches "false negative".

  I'd keep "missed warning". It's the clearer of the two for a non-specialist,
  and the four still read as two matched pairs.

  Shall I apply the paragraph with "false warning", along with the Table 2
  wording?

✻ Cogitated for 6s · done 12:01 PM

❯ what about safe-levels instead of all-clear?  then false safe-levels          

⏺ I'd avoid "safe". It's the same problem we had with "dangerous" in the title,
  in reverse. Water under 100 isn't known to be safe, only below Toronto's
  standard. The City's own green sign says "Beach water quality satisfies
  Toronto's beach water quality standard", not "safe". "False safe-levels" is
  also awkward as a noun for one outcome.

  If "all-clear" doesn't sit well with you, the plainest alternative is "no 
  warning":

  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │                │   Water over the limit on the    │  Water under the   │
  │                │           posting day            │       limit        │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ Warning posted │ correct warning                  │ false warning      │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ No warning     │ missed warning                   │ correct no-warning │
  │ posted         │                                  │                    │
  └────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  This follows what actually happens at the beach: a warning sign is posted, or
  no warning is. It also makes the table headings simple: "Warning posted" and
  "No warning posted".

  My preference is "no warning" over "all-clear", because it doesn't imply the
  water was declared fine. Which do you want?

  ▎ [Beach comparison] Two beaches were over the limit far more often than the 
  ▎ other eight (@fig-beach-days). Marie Curtis Park East was over the limit on 
  ▎ 34% of its sampled days and Sunnyside on 32%, more than seven times the 4% 
  ▎ at Gibraltar Point, the cleanest beach. The other seven beaches ranged from 
  ▎ 16% at Kew Balmy to 7% at Cherry and Hanlan's Point. Sunnyside also stands 
  ▎ out in the shape of its distribution: it is the only beach with more days 
  ▎ between 30 and 100 than below 30, so its typical day is higher, not only its
  ▎ worst. The ranking is not the result of a few bad years. In 19 of the 20 
  ▎ years, both Marie Curtis and Sunnyside were over the limit more often than 
  ▎ every other beach; the exception was 2021, when Kew Balmy (27%) passed 
  ▎ Sunnyside (25%). Nor is it an effect of the reporting ceiling introduced in 
  ▎ 2018: Marie Curtis was over the limit on 37% of days before 2018 and 31% 
  ▎ from 2018, Sunnyside on 32% in both periods, and Gibraltar Point on 5% and 
  ▎ 4%.

  Notes:
  - Opening: it leads with the finding, then gives the numbers as comparisons.
  - The Sunnyside point comes from Figure 3 (738 days in the 30–100 bin against
    616 below 30). It's a new observation, not in your notes, so check whether
    you want it. It supports the idea that Sunnyside is dirtier on ordinary days
    too, but it could be cut for length.
  - The two robustness checks are one sentence each, as your notes suggest.
  - Length: 7 sentences, about 170 words.
