⏺ Here's the proposed [Model estimates] paragraph. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Model estimates] The same-day comparison gives the same ranking 
  ▎ (@fig-beach-estimates). On a typical day, Marie Curtis Park East and 
  ▎ Sunnyside each had about 2.2 times the E. coli of the average beach sampled 
  ▎ that day (95% intervals 2.0 to 2.4 times), whereas Gibraltar Point had 0.66 
  ▎ times. The two worst beaches could not be told apart from each other, but 
  ▎ each differed from every other beach. Of the 45 pairs of beaches, 31 or 32 
  ▎ differed after Holm's correction, depending on how the partial months at 
  ▎ each end of the season were grouped (Appendix [-@sec-appendix-pairs]); most 
  ▎ of the pairs that could not be separated were among the eight cleaner 
  ▎ beaches. Centre Island ranks above Kew Balmy in the model (1.22 against 1.09
  ▎ times) but below it in the share of days over the limit (14% against 16%), 
  ▎ because the model compares typical levels on the same day, whereas the share
  ▎ counts only how often 100 is crossed. The gaps shown are smaller than the 
  ▎ true gaps: in simulated data with known differences, the reporting floor and
  ▎ ceiling reduced the estimated gaps to about 65% of their true size, without
  ▎ reversing any of them (Appendix [-@sec-appendix-validation]).

  Notes:
  - Multiples, not log units. The estimates are given as "times the average
    beach", to match Figure 4's axis. The interval 2.0–2.4 is 10 raised to each
    end of the log interval, for both beaches.
  - The Centre Island and Kew Balmy sentence heads off a reader who compares
    Figure 3 with Figure 4 and sees the order change. It's the one bit of
    explanation in an otherwise factual paragraph.
  - Both appendices are referenced, so the "31 or 32" and the "65%" can be
    checked.
  - Length: 6 sentences, about 190 words.

  Figure 4's caption is still a placeholder. I'd rewrite it along these lines:
  "Each beach's E. coli on a typical day, as a multiple of the average beach
  sampled the same day, with 95% intervals. Beaches whose intervals do not
  overlap differ clearly. The reporting floor and ceiling make these gaps
  smaller than the true ones."

❯ "told apart from each other" is awkward. 0.66 times, or 34% lower?  don't     
  mention 31 or 32, let's commit to one analysis which is to include short      
  months in with the nearest month.                                             

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Here are the three points.

  1. "told apart from each other". Suggested replacement:

  ▎ The two worst beaches did not differ from each other (both 2.19 times), but 
  ▎ each differed clearly from every other beach.

  2. "0.66 times" or "34% lower". I'd write "about a third less (0.66 times)".
  Lay readers take in "a third less" more easily. Keeping 0.66 in brackets
  matches the numbers on Figure 4's axis, and it avoids "34% lower" sitting next
  to the "34%" of days at Marie Curtis.

  3. One analysis, with partial months merged. That's a sound choice. It removes
  the smallest groups (a few days of late May, a few of early September) and
  cuts the number of groups from 96 to 60. It does mean changing the analysis,
  not just the text:

  1. src/toronto_beaches_ecoli/analysis.py: add a grouping column that counts
     May days with June and September days with August, and group the model by
     it. The beach estimates don't change; only the standard errors and
     intervals do, by −4% to +18%, median +6%.
  2. scripts/07-analyse_data.py: drop the calendar-month comparison and its CSV
     (beach-pairwise-grouping-check.csv). Rerun it to regenerate the results
     tables and Figure 4. Result: 31 of 45 pairs differ. Bluffer's against
     Cherry is the pair that drops out.
  3. scripts/06-validate_on_simulated_data.py: rerun it to confirm all ten
     checks still pass with the new grouping.
  4. other/explore/model_explained.ipynb: make the hand calculation use the same
     grouping, and rerun it.
  5. Paper, [Model] paragraph: "calculated from the variation between calendar
     months" becomes "…between months, with the few days of late May and early
     September counted with June and August".
  6. other/notes/results_summary.md: update it so it no longer says "31 or 32".

  The [Model estimates] paragraph would then say "31 of the 45 pairs differed",
  with no hedge.

  Shall I go ahead with all of this, and then show you the revised [Model
  estimates] paragraph?

           verage beach on the same day ($10^{0.3} \approx 2$).

  ▎ [Model estimates] The same-day comparison gives the same ranking 
  ▎ (@fig-beach-estimates). On a typical day, Marie Curtis Park East and 
  ▎ Sunnyside each had about 2.2 times the E. coli of the average beach sampled 
  ▎ that day (95% intervals 2.0 to 2.4 times), whereas Gibraltar Point had about
  ▎ a third less (0.66 times). The two worst beaches did not differ from each 
  ▎ other (both 2.19 times), but each differed clearly from every other beach. 
  ▎ Of the 45 pairs of beaches, 31 differed after Holm's correction (Appendix 
  ▎ [-@sec-appendix-pairs]); the 14 that did not were Marie Curtis and 
  ▎ Sunnyside, Centre Island and Kew Balmy, and 12 pairs among the six cleanest 
  ▎ beaches. Centre Island ranks above Kew Balmy in the model (1.22 against 1.09
  ▎ times) but below it in the share of days over the limit (14% against 16%), 
  ▎ because the model compares typical levels on the same day, whereas the share
  ▎ counts only how often 100 is crossed. The gaps shown are smaller than the 
  ▎ true gaps: in simulated data with known differences, the reporting floor and
  ▎ ceiling reduced the estimated gaps to about 65% of their true size, without
  ▎ reversing any of them (Appendix [-@sec-appendix-validation]).

❯ "differ clearly" can we say "differ significantly"?                           

⏺ Yes, as long as "significantly" is used in its statistical sense and defined
  where it first appears. Before changing the caption, I'll check whether its
  rule, that non-overlapping intervals mean a difference, actually holds after
  Holm's correction.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, but with one change to the Methods, and the check turned up an error in
  the caption.

  In the paragraph: fine. "…but each differed significantly from every other
  beach" is correct. To use the word properly, the Methods should define it
  where the 5% rule is stated. The [Model] sentence would change from "Two
  beaches were judged to differ when…" to:

  ▎ Two beaches were said to differ significantly when a gap as large as the one
  ▎ observed would arise less than 5% of the time if they were in fact alike.

  In the Figure 4 caption: the rule itself is wrong. "Beaches whose intervals do
  not overlap differ clearly" doesn't hold for our results:
  - Bluffer's and Cherry, and Gibraltar Point and Woodbine: their intervals
    don't overlap, but neither pair differs after Holm's correction (corrected p
    = 0.06 and 0.23).
  - Gibraltar Point and Ward's Island: their intervals do overlap, yet they
    differ significantly (corrected p = 0.002).

  This happens because the pairwise test uses the standard error of the
  difference and a stricter threshold after the correction. Overlap of two
  separate intervals is only a rough guide. So I'd drop that sentence from the
  caption, leaving:

  ▎ Each beach's E. coli on a typical day, as a multiple of the average beach 
  ▎ sampled the same day, 2007 to 2026, with 95% intervals. The reporting floor 
  ▎ and ceiling make these gaps smaller than the true ones.

  The Appendix C table then gives the actual pairwise results.

  Apply all three: "differed significantly", the Methods definition, and the
  shorter caption?

           eaches sampled that day, $\beta_b$ is the beach's average gap 
           from the others, and $\varepsilon_{bd}$ is the day-to-day vari

           ge removes anything that affects all beaches at once, such as

⏺ All three changes are in, and the paper renders with no warnings. Nothing is
  committed.

  On Figure 4's x-axis: I'd keep the log scale, for two reasons.

  1. Symmetry. The values are multiples of the average beach. On a log scale,
     "half as much" (0.5×) and "twice as much" (2×) sit the same distance from
     1×. On a linear scale, Gibraltar Point's 0.66× would sit only 0.34 left of
     1×, while Marie Curtis's 2.19× sat 1.19 to the right. The dirty beaches
     would look much further from average than the clean ones really are.
  2. The intervals. They were calculated as estimate ± 1.96 SE in log units, so
     they're symmetric on a log axis. On a linear axis each one would look
     lopsided, longer on the right. That's correct, but distracting.

  The axis label already says "(log scale)", and the tick labels (0.6×, 0.8×,
  1×, 1.25×, 1.5×, 2×, 2.5×) keep it readable without knowing what a logarithm
  is. With a range of only about 0.6× to 2.5×, a linear axis would look
  reasonable, but it would shrink the gaps among the six cleanest beaches.

✻ Cogitated for 20s · done 12:32 PM

❯ OK. and since the graph should be read as "how many times different were      
  these levels from the average" log scale is correct.                          

⏺ Yes, exactly. When the question is "how many times higher or lower", equal
  ratios should be equal distances, and a log axis is what gives that. On it,
  the step from 1× to 2× is the same as from 0.5× to 1×, so the reader's eye
  compares the ratios directly.

  That's also why the model works in log₁₀ units in the first place: differences
  on a log scale are ratios on the original scale.

❯ now yesterday vs today                                                        

⏺ Here's the proposed [Yesterday vs today] paragraph. Every "over" and "under"
  names the posting day, and the numbers come from Table 2. I haven't applied
  it.

  ▎ [Yesterday vs today] Warnings based on the previous day's samples caught 
  ▎ only about a third of the posting days with water over the limit 
  ▎ (@tbl-warning). Of the 2,702 posting days with water over the limit, a 
  ▎ warning was posted on 1,002 (37%); on the other 1,700 the warning was 
  ▎ missed. Of the 2,714 warnings posted, 1,002 (37%) were correct, and the 
  ▎ other 1,712 were false because the posting day's water was under the limit. 
  ▎ Over all pairs, the posting day's water was over the limit on 14% of days; 
  ▎ this rose to 37% when a warning was posted and fell to 10% when none was, so
  ▎ a posted warning made a day over the limit about 3.5 times as likely.

  Notes:
  - Opening: it leads with the main finding, then gives the counts, which match
    the cells of Table 2.
  - The two 37% figures answer different questions: how many bad days were
    caught, and how many warnings were right. Each is stated with its own
    denominator (2,702 and 2,714) so they aren't confused.
  - The last sentence is the comparison your notes asked for (14%, 37%, 10%,
    about 3.5 times). It shows the warning isn't useless, stated as fact without
    commentary.
  - Length: 4 sentences, about 130 words.

❯ for the yesterday vs today, instead of text, what about a bulleted list of    
  derived statistics from the table--things that are relevant to a swimmer. Or  
  maybe a table with a question and then a percentage. Qs: what percentage of   
  the time is the water over the limit but there's No Warning?  How often is    
  the water under the limit when there's a Warning Sign? (to be continued...),  
  and also:                                                                     
                                                                                
  About Beach Water Quality.pdf shows two signs: Unsafe and Safe.  Can't we use 
  those labels since the city uses them?                                        

⏺ Both ideas are good. Some points to settle first, and no changes until you
  say.

  1. A question-and-percentage table. This suits a swimmer better than prose.
  One thing to pin down is that each percentage depends on what you divide by,
  and your first question can be read two ways:

  - "Of all posting days, how often was the water over the limit with no
    warning?" 8.9% (1,700 of 18,998)
  - "When there's no warning, how often is the water over the limit?" 10% (1,700
    of 16,284)

  A swimmer stands in front of a sign, so the second kind, conditional on the
  sign, is the one that matters. Framed that way, a possible set of questions:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────┐
  │                            Question                             │ Answer │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ On any posting day, how often is the water over the limit?      │ 14%    │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ When a warning is posted, how often is the water over the       │ 37%    │
  │ limit?                                                          │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ When a warning is posted, how often is the water actually under │ 63%    │
  │  the limit?                                                     │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ When no warning is posted, how often is the water over the      │ 10%    │
  │ limit?                                                          │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ When the water is over the limit, how often is a warning        │ 37%    │
  │ posted?                                                         │        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────┘

  The 37% and 63% rows are complements, so you might keep only one. I'd keep
  63%, since it's the surprising one. The table would be Table 3, with one short
  sentence of text pointing to it and to Table 2. The rubric still wants tables
  referred to in the text.

  2. "Unsafe" and "Safe". The City's page does label the two signs that way. The
  red one says "WARNING: UNSAFE FOR SWIMMING", and the page captions the blue
  hotline sign "Safe". Using them as the names of the signs, capitalised and
  attributed once to the City, works well and makes the finding sharper:

  ▎ When the "Safe" sign was up, the water was over the limit on 10% of days; 
  ▎ when the "Unsafe" sign was up, it was under the limit on 63%.

  I'd still avoid "safe" as a description of the water itself, as we discussed.
  The sign names are fine because they're the City's words, not the paper's
  claim. It would also mean changing "warning / no warning" throughout, in the
  [Consecutive days] paragraph, Table 2 and this paragraph, to "Unsafe sign /
  Safe sign".

  What other questions did you have in mind for the "to be continued"?

✻ Brewed for 7s · done 12:58 PM

❯ more professional word for beach-goer?                                        

⏺ A few options:

  - "swimmer" is plain, and it's already the word used elsewhere in the paper
    ("water-borne illness among swimmers"). It's my pick.
  - "bather" is the technical term in recreational-water guidelines, and the
    City's own page uses it ("increase the risk of infection to bathers"). It's
    correct, but to a general reader it can sound old-fashioned.
  - "beach user" is what the City's Beach Water Quality page uses ("beach users
    cannot simply rely on only lab results"). It's neutral and covers people who
    wade without swimming.

  I'd use "swimmer", for consistency and because the warning is about going in
  the water. Change it?

  Here's the proposed [Summary] paragraph, which opens the Discussion.
  Commentary is allowed here, and it restates the main conclusion as your class
  notes ask. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Summary] Twenty years of Toronto's beach monitoring give a consistent 
  ▎ picture. Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside were over the limit on about a
  ▎ third of sampled days, more than seven times as often as Gibraltar Point, 
  ▎ and this ranking held from year to year and on both sides of the 2018 change
  ▎ in reporting. Compared on the same days, both carried about twice the E. 
  ▎ coli of the average beach, and because the reporting limits compress the 
  ▎ data, the true gaps are likely larger. The sign posted each day, by 
  ▎ contrast, was a weak guide to that day's water: it missed nearly two thirds 
  ▎ of the days over the limit, and nearly two thirds of the "Warning" signs 
  ▎ went up on days when the water was under the limit. For a swimmer, the 
  ▎ choice of beach therefore says more about the water than the sign posted on 
  ▎ the day.

  Notes:
  - Both findings are covered, each with one or two numbers, and it closes on
    the conclusion shared with the title and subtitle.
  - "Nearly two thirds" twice. It's 63% both times: 1,700 of 2,702 days over the
    limit had a "Safe" sign, and 1,712 of 2,714 "Warning" signs were on days
    under the limit. Table 3 reports the second as 63% and the first as its
    complement, 37% caught. Using "nearly two thirds" in the Discussion avoids
    repeating the percentages.
  - Length: 5 sentences, about 150 words.

  Apply it?

      54  
      55  [Floor and ceiling] The laboratory's reporting limits compress 
          the data at both ends (@fig-raw-results). Results below 10 are 
          rarely reported, so the 49% of results at exactly 10 are best r
          ead as "10 or fewer". From 2018, high results were increasingly
           reported as exactly 1,000; earlier results reached 15,820, whe
          reas none exceeded 1,000 in 2026, which suggests that a reporti
          ng ceiling was introduced. Because both limits narrow the range
          , differences among the cleanest beaches and the severity of th
          e worst days are likely understated. The ceiling can also hide 
          a day over the limit: since 2018, 34 days at a beach included a
           result of 1,000 yet had a daily geometric mean (@sec-data-meth
          ods) below 100, and on about 8 of them a true result of 2,000 o
          r less would have put the day over. The true number of days ove
          r the limit since 2018 may therefore be slightly higher than th
          e number calculated from these data.
      56  
     ...
      118  
      119  [Model] The standard error of each $\beta_b$ was calculated fr
           om the variation between months rather than between days, beca
           use a wet week raises a beach's levels for several days runnin
           g. The few sampling days in late May and early September were 
           counted with June and August. Grouping days this way roughly d
           oubles the width of the intervals compared with treating every
            day as independent. Each estimate is reported with a 95% inte
           rval: if the analysis were repeated over many different runs o
           f summers, about 95% of such intervals would contain the true 
           gap. Two beaches were said to differ significantly when a gap 
           as large as the one observed would arise less than 5% of the t
           ime if they were in fact alike. Testing all 45 pairs this way 
           would produce about two false differences by chance, so the co
           mparisons were corrected by Holm's method, which raises the ba
           r each comparison must clear so that the chance of any false d
           ifference stays near 5%.

    limit, so "water over the limit" is the natural shorthand.

  I've added this distinction to your saved style notes. Nothing is committed.

⏺ Thank you. Committed as 7ffcd13, and nothing is left uncommitted.

  Here's the proposed [Why beaches differ] paragraph, the next in the
  Discussion. It draws on Saleem et al. and Sanchez et al., and hedges
  throughout, since the paper can't test these reasons. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Why beaches differ] The two worst beaches share a location at the mouth of 
  ▎ a river. Marie Curtis Park East lies at the mouth of Etobicoke Creek, and 
  ▎ Sunnyside lies beside the mouth of the Humber River, where a breakwall 
  ▎ shelters the beach from the waves of the open lake [@saleem2023]. Both 
  ▎ rivers drain urban land, and higher stream flow has been associated with 
  ▎ higher E. coli at Toronto beaches [@sanchez2021]; at Sunnyside, the 
  ▎ breakwall may also hold river water near the shore rather than letting it 
  ▎ disperse. By contrast, three of the four island beaches, which face the open
  ▎ lake away from any river mouth, were among the cleanest; Centre Island was 
  ▎ the exception. These explanations remain hypotheses that the present 
  ▎ analysis does not test. Two analyses of these data could test them: 
  ▎ comparing the sites within each beach, since sites nearer a river mouth 
  ▎ should have higher results if the river is the source, and linking daily 
  ▎ results to river flow and rainfall, to see whether the gap between beaches 
  ▎ widens after heavy rain.

  Notes:
  - Goal-first order: the observation (both worst beaches are at river mouths),
    then the evidence from the literature, a hedged mechanism ("may also hold"),
    the contrast (the island beaches), and finally how to test it.
  - The site-comparison test uses data already in this dataset: the site codes
    and coordinates. That makes it a concrete next step rather than a vague one.
  - Left out: Woodbine is about 1 km from the Ashbridges Bay treatment plant
    [Young et al.] yet is fairly clean (9%). It's interesting, but it would need
    its own explanation.
  - Length: 6 sentences, about 170 words.

  Apply it?

⏺ Applied and committed as 8fa4e8b.

  Here's the proposed [Improving warnings] paragraph. It uses the City's two
  pages and Saleem et al. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Improving warnings] The weakness of the daily sign follows from the one-day
  ▎ delay: water quality changes faster than the testing can follow, so a sign 
  ▎ based on the previous day's samples misses most days over the limit and puts
  ▎ up most of its warnings on days under it. The City acknowledges this, 
  ▎ advising swimmers to stay out of the water for 24 to 48 hours after heavy 
  ▎ rain and when the water is cloudy [@tphbeachwq]. Two approaches could narrow
  ▎ the gap. The first is a predictive model that estimates the day's water 
  ▎ quality from conditions such as rainfall, wind direction and turbidity; 
  ▎ Toronto Public Health piloted one with Environment Canada at Marie Curtis 
  ▎ Park East and Sunnyside in 2022, but had suspended it by 2025 [@tphabout]. 
  ▎ The second is same-day testing. At the same two beaches, a qPCR method, 
  ▎ which measures the DNA of Enterococcus, another faecal bacterium, within a 
  ▎ few hours, indicated that 5% of the City's posting decisions had missed a 
  ▎ health risk and 23% had closed the beach unnecessarily [@saleem2023]. 
  ▎ Because these are the beaches with the most days over the limit, they are 
  ▎ also where a faster or predictive warning would help swimmers most.

  Notes:
  - Opening: it explains why the sign performs poorly (the one-day delay), then
    gives the two fixes, each with a source.
  - The City's own advice is included: stay out for 24–48 hours after rain, or
    when the water is cloudy. It shows the City recognises the delay.
  - The predictive-model pilot is from the City's page. It ran in 2022 at
    exactly our two worst beaches and has since been suspended, which ties
    neatly to this paper's finding.
  - qPCR is defined in a clause, and the numbers are reported as Saleem et al.
    framed them. Their comparison was against the City's actual postings, not
    our computed signs.
  - Length: 6 sentences, about 190 words.

  Apply it?

⏺ Committed as 08a8e25, with your wording ("…on days under the limit").

  Here's the proposed [Weaknesses] paragraph. It covers the four points in the
  outline. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Weaknesses] Four limitations qualify these results. First, the signs in 
  ▎ this paper were reconstructed from the sample results rather than taken from
  ▎ the City's records of what was posted. Because the City may base its 
  ▎ decisions on a geometric mean over two days [@saleem2023], the signs 
  ▎ actually posted may have been right more or less often than the 
  ▎ reconstructed ones. Second, the reporting floor at 10 and ceiling at 1,000 
  ▎ compress the data, so the gaps between beaches are understated; in simulated
  ▎ data, only about 65% of a true gap survived. Third, the analysis uses no 
  ▎ weather data, so it cannot say how much of the day-to-day variation, or of 
  ▎ the difference between beaches, follows from rainfall and wind. Fourth, 
  ▎ Sunnyside has four sites where most beaches have five, which makes its daily
  ▎ geometric mean noisier and slightly more likely to cross the limit; in 
  ▎ simulated data this added well under one percentage point to its share of 
  ▎ days over the limit, far too little to explain its 32%.

  Notes:
  - Each weakness comes with its consequence and, where there is one, its size
    (65%; under one percentage point).
  - The first weakness is split into two sentences, following your preference
    for shorter sentences.
  - The ceiling is covered by "compress the data", with a short link back to the
    Measurement section, rather than repeating the hidden-days detail.
  - Length: 7 sentences, about 180 words.

  Apply it?

⏺ Here's the proposed Appendix A, in two paragraphs plus the footnote on the
  City's reply. All the counts come from the raw and cleaned files. I also ran
  the test suite on the cleaned data just now, and all checks pass. I haven't
  applied it.

  ▎ [Cleaning details] The data were downloaded from Open Data Toronto on 22 
  ▎ September 2026 [@tphdata] and contain 102,534 rows, one for each site on 
  ▎ each sampling day from 3 June 2007 to 16 September 2026. Of these, 3,543 
  ▎ (3.5%) have no result, usually because no sample could be taken, which 
  ▎ leaves 98,991 results. Three groups of results were then removed. The first 
  ▎ is a single reading of 6,191,768 E. coli per 100 mL at Marie Curtis Park 
  ▎ East on 15 June 2009, about 390 times the next highest result in the record 
  ▎ (15,820), which was treated as an error. The second is the 220 results from 
  ▎ sites 60W and GP6, both added on 19 May 2026: they are listed under 
  ▎ Sunnyside and Gibraltar Point but lie 14 to 16 km away, between Kew Balmy 
  ▎ and Bluffer's Park, whereas every other site lies within 0.4 km of the rest 
  ▎ of its beach.^[On 25 September 2026, Toronto Public Health staff said that 
  ▎ the listing of these two sites had been referred for correction (personal 
  ▎ communication). This paper uses the data as downloaded on 22 September 2026,
  ▎ so a later correction does not change its results, but a new download may 
  ▎ list these sites differently.] The third is 400 results from dates outside 
  ▎ the sampling season, taken here as Victoria Day to Labour Day; the City 
  ▎ describes the season as June to Labour Day [@tphabout], but in most years 
  ▎ since 2012 sampling has begun just after Victoria Day. The remaining 98,370 
  ▎ results come from 50 sites on 2,019 days.
  ▎
  ▎ [Cleaning details] No other values were changed. Results at the reporting 
  ▎ floor of 10 and at the ceiling of 1,000 were kept as reported, as were the 
  ▎ 63 results below 10. For the analysis by day, the 50 beach-days with fewer 
  ▎ than four results were left out, leaving 19,662 beach-days. The cleaned data
  ▎ were checked with automated tests written with pointblank [@pointblank], 
  ▎ covering the columns and their types, the ranges of dates and values, one 
  ▎ result per site per day, and the number of sites at each beach.

  Notes:
  - The footnote follows points_for_the_paper.md: no staff name, the download
    date given, and a warning that a fresh download may differ. Quarto numbers
    it automatically.
  - Only in this appendix: the 50 beach-days dropped for having fewer than four
    results, and the fact that values at the floor and ceiling were kept
    unchanged.
  - The test suite is described by what it checks, not by a count of checks.
  - Length: about 330 words, fine for an appendix.

  Apply it?

⏺ Here's the proposed Appendix B. It's based on method_validation.md, the
  simulation design in analysis_plan.md, and a fresh run of
  scripts/06-validate_on_simulated_data.py just now, in which all ten checks
  pass. It's two paragraphs and one small table. I haven't applied it.

  ▎ [Simulation check] To confirm that the analysis finds real differences and 
  ▎ does not invent them, it was first run on simulated data in which the answer
  ▎ is known. The simulation reproduces the structure of the real data: the 
  ▎ same ten beaches and numbers of sites, daily sampling from Victoria Day to 
  ▎ Labour Day, 2007 to 2026, and results built on the log scale from a beach 
  ▎ baseline, a city-wide daily effect shared by all beaches, a daily effect for
  ▎ each beach, and noise for each sample. Both daily effects carry half of the
  ▎ previous day's value into the next, so that wet spells last several days. 
  ▎ Six beaches were built at a common level, two were raised by 0.2 on the 
  ▎ log10 scale (about 1.6 times) and two by 0.4 (about 2.5 times). The 
  ▎ simulated results were then reported the way the laboratory reports real 
  ▎ ones: rounded to multiples of 10, with nothing below 10, and, from 2018, 
  ▎ most results above 1,000 reported as 1,000.
  ▎
  ▎ [Simulation check] The analysis recovered what was built in 
  ▎ (@tbl-simulation). Without the reporting floor and ceiling, the estimated 
  ▎ gaps were 0.399 and 0.208, against true values of 0.400 and 0.200. With 
  ▎ them, the estimates fell to 0.260 and 0.126, about 65% of the true gaps, 
  ▎ while the order of the groups was unchanged. All 12 comparisons between a 
  ▎ beach raised by 0.4 and a beach at the common level were found to differ 
  ▎ significantly, and none of the 15 comparisons between beaches built at the 
  ▎ same level was, so Holm's correction kept false differences in check. 
  ▎ Sunnyside and Kew Balmy, with four and six sites, were both built at the 
  ▎ common level, so their shares of days over the limit show the effect of the 
  ▎ number of sites alone: at 4.2% and 4.0%, both fell within the range of the 
  ▎ other common beaches (4.4% to 6.2%).
  ▎
  ▎ |                                          | Beaches raised by 0.4 | Beaches
  ▎ raised by 0.2 |
  ▎ |------------------------------------------|-----------------------|--------
  ▎ ---------------|
  ▎ | Built into the simulation                |                 0.400 |        
  ▎         0.200 |
  ▎ | Estimated, without the floor and ceiling |                 0.399 |        
  ▎         0.208 |
  ▎ | Estimated, with the floor and ceiling    |                 0.260 |        
  ▎         0.126 |
  ▎
  ▎ : Gaps above the six beaches built at a common level, on the log10 scale, in
  ▎ simulated data. The reporting floor and ceiling shrink both gaps to about 
  ▎ 65% of their true size without changing their order. {#tbl-simulation}

  Notes:
  - The site-count check is now given with the actual numbers from the
    simulation. It supports the [Weaknesses] claim: Sunnyside's four sites don't
    push its share above the other common beaches.
  - The table is typed in, not computed, because script 06 prints these numbers
    but doesn't save them. I can have 06 save them to other/results/ and the
    paper read that file, like the other tables. That's more reproducible, but
    it's one more code change.
  - A stale number in the notes: method_validation.md still says the real data's
    standard-error ratio is 1.90. With merged months it's 2.0. I'll fix that
    note in passing.

  Apply it, with the table typed in or saved by the script?

⏺ Appendix C is in, and the paper renders with no warnings.

  Paragraph:

  ▎ [Pairwise table] Table 5 lists all 45 comparisons between pairs of beaches. 
  ▎ For each pair, the higher beach is listed first, and the ratio is how many 
  ▎ times higher it was than the other beach on the same day: 10 raised to the 
  ▎ difference between their $\beta_b$ values in Equation 1. The corrected 
  ▎ p-value is the chance of a gap at least as large if the two beaches were in 
  ▎ fact alike, after Holm's correction for making 45 comparisons; pairs with a 
  ▎ corrected p-value below 0.05 differ significantly.

  Table 5 reads other/results/beach-pairwise-differences.csv and has five
  columns: Higher beach, Lower beach, Ratio, Corrected p, Differs. It uses the
  short beach names from Table 1 and is sorted from the largest ratio (Sunnyside
  against Gibraltar Point, 3.30) to the smallest (Marie Curtis against
  Sunnyside, 1.00, not significant). Very small p-values print as "<0.001". The
  table runs over a page break, and the heading row repeats on the new page.

  This is also the first place the paper says "p-value" in so many words. It's
  defined in the paragraph, matching the Methods definition of "differ
  significantly".

  Nothing is committed. Shall I commit it?

❯ does appendix c add much?                                                     

⏺ Not much, in my view. What it offers is completeness: exact ratios and
  p-values for all 45 pairs, so a reader can check any single comparison. But
  the paper already carries the substance:

  - The [Model estimates] paragraph gives the count (31 of 45) and names all 14
    pairs that don't differ.
  - Figure 4 shows every beach's ratio with its interval, so any pair's ratio
    can be read off roughly.
  - The full table is already public in the repo, as
    other/results/beach-pairwise-differences.csv.

  Against that, it costs about a page and a half of mostly "<0.001, Yes" rows,
  and it brings p-values into the paper for the first time. You were already
  worried about length.

  I'd cut it. The [Model estimates] reference "(Appendix C)" would then become a
  pointer to the file in the repo, something like "(the full table of pairs is
  in the paper's repository)", or be dropped. The paper's footnote already links
  the repository. Cut it?

⏺ The Introduction and abstract are written, applied and rendered, with no

  Abstract (four sentences, following your class recipe):
  1. The area: Toronto tests daily and posts a warning when E. coli exceeds the
     limit.
  2. The data and method: 98,370 results from 2007 to 2026, comparing the
     beaches and the previous-day sign.
  3. The headline: Marie Curtis and Sunnyside were over the limit on about a
     third of days, more than seven times the cleanest beach; the sign missed
     nearly two thirds of the days over the limit.
  4. The implication: the choice of beach says more about the water quality than
     the sign on the day.

  Introduction, four new paragraphs after your [Why E. coli] paragraph:
  - [Questions and gap]: Toronto's daily sampling and signs; the two questions,
    each put as a swimmer would ask it; and the gap. Sanchez et al. and Saleem
    et al. studied weather and same-day testing, but neither compared all ten
    beaches over the full record or measured how often the daily sign is right.
  - [Findings]: the numbers: 34% and 32% against 4%, 19 of 20 years, 2.2 times
    the average beach, and the sign results (10% and 63%).
  - [Why it matters]: the choice of beach against the sign; and the two worst
    beaches are where faster warnings would help most, which ties to the City's
    pilot and to Saleem et al.
  - [Roadmap]: each section and both appendices, with cross-references.

  Two corrections I made after drafting:
  - "tested same-day testing" is now "evaluated same-day testing".
  - "a same-day test has been shown to reduce both missed and false warnings" is
    now "could reduce". Saleem et al. only claim the potential, so the first
    version overstated them.

  For you to check:
  - The opening of [Questions and gap] repeats some of [Data source], on daily
    sampling and the 100-against-200 limit. The Introduction has to stand on its
    own, so some overlap is expected, but you may want to trim the Data
    section's version.
  - I noticed you changed the title to "…Are Some Beaches Less Safe Than
    Others?". The abstract and the Introduction's closing both fit it.

  What's left is the final pass:
  - removing the [tags],
  - layout (figure positions and page breaks),
  - the claims you wanted to check,
  - deleting the z_ figures,
  - the README,
  - a last check for typos and banned words.

⏺ Those are the better pair. Both are about the "Warning" sign, and together
  they say it misses most bad days and is wrong most of the time it's up. Two
  small fixes:

  1. "(i.e. "Safe")": I'd drop it. It uses "Safe" to describe the water, which
     we decided against, since "Safe" is only the name of the sign. It also
     blurs the point, because on those days the "Safe" sign wasn't up. "Under
     the limit" already says it.
  2. "was posted only 37% of the days" is a little loose. "On only 37% of those
     days" makes clear which days are meant.

  Together:

  ▎ The daily sign, by contrast, was a weak guide: on the days when the water 
  ▎ was over the limit, the "Warning" sign was up on only 37% of them, and when 
  ▎ the "Warning" sign was up, the water was under the limit on 63% of days.

  the limit", since 37% caught means 63% missed. Apply it?

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You're right. The last sentence switches from why the findings matter to a
  list of what others have tried, and that belongs in the Discussion, where
  [Improving warnings] already covers it.

  A stronger ending would join the paper's two findings, and there's a number
  that does exactly that. I just computed how often the water was over the limit
  on days when the "Safe" sign was up, beach by beach:

  ┌────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         Beach          │ Water over the limit under a "Safe" sign │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Marie Curtis Park East │ 27%                                      │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sunnyside              │ 25%                                      │
  ├────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Gibraltar Point        │ 4%                                       │
  └────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The two findings compound: at the worst beaches, even a "Safe" sign leaves
  about one day in four over the limit. Proposed rewrite:

  ▎ [Why it matters] These findings matter to anyone deciding where and when to 
  ▎ swim. The choice of beach says more about the likely water quality than the 
  ▎ sign posted on the day, because the sign describes water that has since 
  ▎ changed. The two effects also compound: at Marie Curtis Park East and 
  ▎ Sunnyside, the water was over the limit on about one day in four even when 
  ▎ the "Safe" sign was up, compared with one day in 25 at Gibraltar Point. A 
  ▎ swimmer at those two beaches is therefore better guided by the beach's 
  ▎ record than by the sign, and those are the beaches where a faster warning 
  ▎ would protect the most people.

  One knock-on change: the Introduction shouldn't give a number that isn't in
  the Results. So I'd add one sentence to [Yesterday vs today] with the same
  figures, for example: "The sign was least reliable where it mattered most:
  under a "Safe" sign, the water was over the limit on 27% of days at Marie
  Curtis Park East and 25% at Sunnyside, against 4% at Gibraltar Point." For
  reproducibility, script 07 would also save these per-beach shares to
  other/results/.

  Apply both, and the script change?

❯ when you say "a faster warning would make the most difference" do we mention  
  earlier that that's an option? I didn't see qPCR mentioned in the intro       

⏺ You're right, that isn't set up. The Introduction does mention same-day
  testing once, in [Questions and gap] ("…have evaluated same-day testing at two
  beaches over one summer"), but only as other people's work. Nothing says a
  faster warning is possible or what it would look like, so the last sentence of
  [Why it matters] assumes something the reader hasn't been told.

  I'd make the sentence say what the faster warning would be, and point to where
  it's discussed, without bringing qPCR into the Introduction. It's a technical
  term, and the Discussion explains it properly:

  ▎ A swimmer at those two beaches is therefore better guided by the beach's 
  ▎ record than by the sign, and those are the beaches where a faster warning, 
  ▎ from same-day testing or a prediction based on the weather, would protect 
  ▎ the most people (@sec-discussion).

  That names both options in plain words. It also links back to the earlier
  mention of same-day testing and forward to [Improving warnings], where both
  are explained with sources. Apply it?

❯ figure 1. does Esri need a reference?                                         

⏺ Yes, I'd add one. The rubric asks for "all data, software, literature, and any
  other relevant material" to be cited and listed. The basemap is material the
  paper didn't make, so "Map tiles from Esri" in the caption isn't quite enough
  on its own. Esri's terms are already met, because its credit ("Tiles (C) Esri
  — Esri, DeLorme, NAVTEQ") prints on the map.

  The fix is a reference entry plus a citation in the caption. contextily only
  downloads the tiles, and it's already cited separately:

  bibtex
  @misc{esri,
    title = {{World Light Gray Canvas Base}},
    author = {{Esri}},
    year = {2026},
    note = {Map tiles. Sources: Esri, DeLorme, NAVTEQ},
    url = {https://server.arcgisonline.com/ArcGIS/rest/services/Canvas/World_Lig
  ht_Gray_Base/MapServer},
    urldate = {2026-09-26}
  }

  The caption would end "…Map tiles from Esri [n]." The URL is the tile service
  that contextily's Esri gray canvas actually downloads from.

  Add it?

      54  
      55  [Floor and ceiling] The laboratory's reporting limits compress 
          the data at both ends (@fig-raw-results). Results below 10 are 
          rarely reported, so the 49% of results at exactly 10 are best r
          ead as "10 or fewer". From 2018, high results were increasingly
           reported as exactly 1,000; earlier results reached 15,820, whe
          reas none exceeded 1,000 in 2026, which suggests that a reporti
          ng ceiling was introduced. Because both limits narrow the range
          , differences among the cleanest beaches and the severity of th
          e worst days are likely understated. The ceiling can also hide 
          a day over the limit: since 2018, 34 days at a beach included a
           result of 1,000 yet had a daily geometric mean (@sec-data-meth
          ods) below 100, and on about 8 of them a true result of 2,000 o
          r less would have put the day over. The true number of days ove
          r the limit since 2018 may therefore be slightly higher than th
          e number calculated from these data.
      56

         -is one water sample (98,991 in total), before any cleaning. The
         - vertical axis is logarithmic, so each gridline is 100 times th
         -e one below. Points are spread sideways within each year so tha
         -t they can be seen; the *E. coli* count itself is never moved. 
         -Most results are reported in multiples of 10, which is why the 
         -low readings fall into separate rows. The laboratory reports no
         - results as zero, and "no colonies detected" is assumed to be r

         -. The dashed line marks 100 *E. coli* per 100 mL, the level at

⏺ Committed as e8f27ac.

  You're right to expect that, and I do keep your changes, as long as they're
  saved. Every edit I make starts by reading paper.qmd from disk, changes only
  the paragraph in question, and writes it back, so everything you've saved is
  kept.

  What I can't see is an edit that's still only in PyCharm's editor and not yet
  saved. If PyCharm then notices that I've changed the file on disk, it reloads
  the file, and the unsaved edit can be lost. That's the most likely explanation
  here.

  Two ways to prevent it:
  - In PyCharm: Settings › Appearance & Behavior › System Settings. Make sure
    "Save files if the IDE is idle for" is ticked, with a short delay such as
    1–2 seconds. Then your edits reach the disk almost immediately.
  - On my side: before any edit to paper.qmd, I'll check git diff for your
    changes and mention them. If something you meant to change isn't there,
    we'll catch it before I write.

      119  [Model] The standard error of each $\beta_b$ was calculated fr
           om the variation between months rather than between days, beca
           use a wet week raises a beach's levels for several days runnin
           g. The few sampling days in late May and early September were 
           counted with June and August. Grouping days this way roughly d
           oubles the width of the intervals compared with treating every
            day as independent. Each estimate is reported with a 95% inte
           rval: if the analysis were repeated over many different runs o
           f summers, about 95% of such intervals would contain the true 
           gap. Two beaches were said to differ significantly when a gap 
           as large as the one observed would arise less than 5% of the t
           ime if they were in fact alike. Testing all 45 pairs this way 
           would produce about two false differences by chance, so the co
           mparisons were corrected by Holm's method, which raises the ba
           r each comparison must clear so that the chance of any false d
           ifference stays near 5%.

✻ Crunched for 22s · done 3:12 PM

❯ Each beach’s E. coli on a typical day. what does typical day mean?            

⏺ It's vague. What the estimate actually is: for each day, the beach's level
  divided by the average of all beaches sampled that day, then averaged over all
  ~2,000 days (on the log scale, so it's a geometric average). "Typical day"
  was trying to say "averaged over days", but a reader could take it to mean a
  median day, or a day with nothing unusual about it.

  "Typical day" appears in two places, and I'd replace it in both:

  1. Figure 4 caption:
  ▎ Each beach's E. coli, averaged over all sampled days, as a multiple of the 
  average beach sampled the same day, 2007 to 2026, with 95% intervals. The 
  reporting floor and ceiling make these gaps smaller than the true ones.
  2. [Model estimates], second sentence:
  ▎ On average, Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside each had about 2.2 times 
  the E. coli of the average beach sampled the same day (95% intervals 2.0 to 
  2.4 times), whereas Gibraltar Point had about a third less (0.66 times).

  Apply both?

      133  [Yesterday vs today] @tbl-warning compares the sign posted at 
           each beach with the water quality measured on the posting day,
            for 18,998 pairs of consecutive days. The rows give the sign,
            "Warning" or "Safe", which is based on the previous day's sam
           ples; the columns give whether the posting day's water was ove
           r or under Toronto's limit. The sign was right on 1,002 days w
           ith a "Warning" sign and water over the limit, and on 14,584 d
           ays with a "Safe" sign and water under it. It was wrong on 1,7
           12 days with a "Warning" sign and water under the limit, and o
           n 1,700 days with a "Safe" sign and water over it. @tbl-sign-q
           uestions gives percentages derived from these counts, as answe
           rs to questions relevant to a Toronto beach swimmer. The sign 
           was least reliable where it mattered most: under a "Safe" sign
           , the water was over the limit on 27% of days at Marie Curtis 
           Park East and 25% at Sunnyside, against 4% at Gibraltar Point.

⏺ I agree, remove it. The two questions are about the same days, the ones with a
  "Warning" sign up, so 37% and 63% must add to 100%, and the table only needs
  one of them. Keep the third question (63% under the limit), for two reasons:

  - Removing the second question also gets rid of a coincidence that could
    confuse readers: it and the last question both come out at 37%, from
    different denominators (2,714 "Warning" days and 2,702 days over the limit).

  The text doesn't cite the 37% from that question anywhere. The Introduction's
  37% is the last question's figure, so nothing else needs to change. Remove it?

      52  
      53  The laboratory's reporting limits compress the data at both end
          s (@fig-raw-results). Results below 10 are rarely reported, so 
          the 49% of results at exactly 10 are best read as "10 or fewer"
          . From 2018, high results were increasingly reported as exactly
           1,000; earlier results reached 15,820, whereas none exceeded 1
          ,000 in 2026, which suggests that a reporting ceiling was intro
          duced. Because both limits narrow the range, differences among 
          the cleanest beaches and the severity of the worst days are lik
          ely understated. The ceiling can also hide a day over the limit
          : since 2018, 34 days at a beach included a result of 1,000 yet
           had a daily geometric mean (@sec-data-methods) below 100, and 
          on about 8 of them a true result of 2,000 or less would have pu
          t the day over. The true number of days over the limit since 20
          18 may therefore be slightly higher than the number calculated 
          from these data.

      106 +                                                              
      107  ## Methods {#sec-data-methods}
      108  
      109  Because Toronto's limit applies to the geometric mean of a bea
           ch's results on a day rather than to any single result [@young
           2023], the analysis works with beach-days. The geometric mean 
           of $n$ results is the $n$th root of their product, or equivale
           ntly the average of their logarithms converted back. It suits 
           counts that span orders of magnitude, because a single extreme
            result does not dominate it: results of 10, 10, 10, 10 and 1,
           000 give a geometric mean of 25, against an ordinary average o
           f 208. A beach-day was counted only when it had at least four 
           results, rather than five, so that Sunnyside (four sites) is i
           ncluded. This gives 19,662 beach-days, of which 2,816 (14%) we
           re over the limit. A day over the limit is not necessarily a d
           ay on which a warning was posted---the data do not record post
           ings. Saleem et al. describe posting decisions based on a geom
           etric mean over two days [@saleem2023]. In addition, a posted 
           warning reflects the previous day's water quality, because tes
           ting takes about 24 hours (@sec-data-measurement).

      123  Two beaches were over the limit far more often than the other

      126 -                                                              
      125  The same-day comparison gives the same ranking (@fig-beach-est
           imates). On average, Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside each
            had about 2.2 times the *E. coli* of the average beach sample
           d the same day (95% intervals 2.0 to 2.4 times), whereas Gibra
           ltar Point had about a third less (0.66 times). The two worst 
           beaches did not differ from each other (both 2.19 times), but 
           each differed significantly from every other beach. Of the 45 
           pairs of beaches, 31 differed after Holm's correction; the 14 
           that did not were Marie Curtis and Sunnyside, Centre Island an
           d Kew Balmy, and 12 pairs among the six cleanest beaches. Cent
           re Island ranks above Kew Balmy in the model (1.22 against 1.0
           9 times) but below it in the share of days over the limit (14%
            against 16%), because the model compares typical levels on th
           e same day, whereas the share counts only how often 100 is cro
           ssed. The gaps shown are smaller than the true gaps: in simula
           ted data with known differences, the reporting floor and ceili
           ng reduced the estimated gaps to about 65% of their true size,
            without reversing any of them (Appendix [-@sec-appendix-valid

      175  # Discussion {#sec-discussion}
      176  
      177  Twenty years of Toronto's beach monitoring give a consistent p
           icture. Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside were over the lim
           it on about a third of sampled days, more than seven times as 
           often as Gibraltar Point, and this ranking held from year to y
           ear and on both sides of the 2018 change in reporting. Compare
           d on the same days, both carried about twice the *E. coli* of 
           the average beach, and because the reporting limits compress t
           he data, the true gaps are likely larger. The sign posted each
            day, by contrast, was a weak guide to that day's water qualit
           y: it missed nearly two thirds of the days over the limit, and
            nearly two thirds of the "Warning" signs went up on days when
            the water was under the limit. For a swimmer, the choice of b
           each therefore says more about the water quality than the sign
            posted on the day.

     that page more than half empty.

     the tables.

     page break. A small LaTeX rule (needspace, added to the header) now moves
     it whole to the next page when it doesn't fit.

     last few lines had sat almost alone on a nearly empty page. The References
     still start on a new page.

      58  The laboratory's reporting limits compress the data at both end
          s (@fig-raw-results). Results below 10 are rarely reported, so 
          the 49% of results at exactly 10 are best read as "10 or fewer"
          . From 2018, high results were increasingly reported as exactly
           1,000; earlier results reached 15,820, whereas none exceeded 1
          ,000 in 2026, which suggests that a reporting ceiling was intro
          duced. Because both limits narrow the range, differences among 
          the cleanest beaches and the severity of the worst days are lik
          ely understated. The ceiling can also hide a day over the limit
          : since 2018, 34 days at a beach included a result of 1,000 yet
           had a daily geometric mean (@sec-data-methods) below 100, and 
          on about 8 of them a true result of 2,000 or less would have pu
          t the day over. The true number of days over the limit since 20
          18 may therefore be slightly higher than the number calculated 
          from these data.

      112  ## Methods {#sec-data-methods}
      113  
      114  Because Toronto's limit applies to the geometric mean of a bea
           ch's results on a day rather than to any single result [@young
           2023], the analysis works with beach-days. The geometric mean 
           of $n$ results is the $n$th root of their product, or equivale
           ntly the average of their logarithms converted back. It suits 
           counts that span orders of magnitude, because a single extreme
            result does not dominate it: results of 10, 10, 10, 10 and 1,
           000 give a geometric mean of 25, against an ordinary average o
           f 208. A beach-day was counted only when it had at least four 
           results, rather than five, so that Sunnyside (four sites) is i
           ncluded. This gives 19,662 beach-days, of which 2,816 (14%) we
           re over the limit. A day over the limit is not necessarily a d
           ay on which a warning was posted---the data do not record post
           ings. Saleem et al. describe posting decisions based on a geom
           etric mean over two days [@saleem2023]. In addition, a posted 
           warning reflects the previous day's water quality, because tes
           ting takes about 24 hours (@sec-data-measurement).

      128  Two beaches were over the limit far more often than the other 
           eight (@fig-beach-days). Marie Curtis Park East was over the l
           imit on 34% of its sampled days and Sunnyside on 32%, more tha
           n seven times the 4% at Gibraltar Point, the cleanest beach. T
           he other seven beaches ranged from 16% at Kew Balmy to 7% at C
           herry and Hanlan's Point. Sunnyside also stands out in the sha
           pe of its distribution: it is the only beach with more days be
           tween 30 and 100 than below 30, so its typical day is higher, 
           not only its worst. The ranking is not the result of a few bad
            years. In 19 of the 20 years, both Marie Curtis and Sunnyside
            were over the limit more often than every other beach; the ex
           ception was 2021, when Kew Balmy (27%) passed Sunnyside (25%).
            Nor is it an effect of the reporting ceiling introduced in 20
           18: Marie Curtis was over the limit on 37% of days before 2018
            and 31% from 2018, Sunnyside on 32% in both periods, and Gibr
           altar Point on 5% and 4%.

      132  The same-day comparison gives the same ranking (@fig-beach-est
           imates). On average, Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside each
            had about 2.2 times the *E. coli* of the average beach sample
           d the same day (95% intervals 2.0 to 2.4 times), whereas Gibra
           ltar Point had about a third less (0.66 times). The two worst 
           beaches did not differ from each other (both 2.19 times), but 
           each differed significantly from every other beach. Of the 45 
           pairs of beaches, 31 differed after Holm's correction; the 14 
           that did not were Marie Curtis and Sunnyside, Centre Island an
           d Kew Balmy, and 12 pairs among the six cleanest beaches. Cent
           re Island ranks above Kew Balmy in the model (1.22 against 1.0
           9 times) but below it in the share of days over the limit (14%
            against 16%), because the model compares typical levels on th
           e same day, whereas the share counts only how often 100 is cro
           ssed. The gaps shown are smaller than the true gaps: in simula
           ted data with known differences, the reporting floor and ceili
           ng reduced the estimated gaps to about 65% of their true size,
            without reversing any of them (Appendix [-@sec-appendix-valid

      182  # Discussion {#sec-discussion}
      183  
      184  Twenty years of Toronto's beach monitoring give a consistent p
           icture. Marie Curtis Park East and Sunnyside were over the lim
           it on about a third of sampled days, more than seven times as 
           often as Gibraltar Point, and this ranking held from year to y
           ear and on both sides of the 2018 change in reporting. Compare
           d on the same days, both carried about twice the *E. coli* of 
           the average beach, and because the reporting limits compress t
           he data, the true gaps are likely larger. The sign posted each
            day, by contrast, was a weak guide to that day's water qualit
           y: it missed nearly two thirds of the days over the limit, and
            nearly two thirds of the "Warning" signs went up on days when
            the water was under the limit. For a swimmer, the choice of b
           each therefore says more about the water quality than the sign
            posted on the day.

      43  
      44  To reduce water-borne illness among swimmers, Toronto Public He
          alth, Toronto Water, the police Marine Unit and the City's park
          s division jointly sample the water at the city's ten supervise
          d beaches every day from Victoria Day to Labour Day [@tphdata].
           Samples are taken at four to six fixed sites along each beach 
          (@fig-sites-map), and when the laboratory count of *E. coli* at
           a beach is too high, Toronto Public Health posts signs warning
           against swimming [@tphbeachwq]. The results are published on t
          he City's open data portal [@tphdata; @opendatatoronto] and for
          m the data for this paper.

         -per uses. The two orange points, sites 60W and GP6, were added 
         -in 2026 and sit 14 to 16 km from the beaches they are listed un
         -der, so they are left out. Map tiles from Esri [@esri].](figure
         -s/sampling-sites-map.png){#fig-sites-map}                      
      47 -                                                               
      46  Toronto's standard of 100 *E. coli* per 100 mL is half that of 
          Ontario and Health Canada: Health Canada raised its guideline f
          rom 100 to 200 in 2012, and Ontario follows it, whereas Toronto
           retained the earlier, stricter value [@tphabout; @sanchez2021]
          . Because 100 is the level at which Toronto posts warnings, it 
          is the limit used throughout this paper.

      50  Because the data describe water quality rather than people, the
          y raise no privacy concerns; the ethical stakes lie instead in

          s status to be calculated the same way for all twenty years.

❯ should Figure X, Table X be bold?                                             

⏺ In captions, bold labels are common and look good. ACS journals, for example,
  set "Figure 1." in bold at the start of each caption. It helps the reader find
  where each caption starts, especially under a busy figure like Figure 3.

  In the running text, references like "(Figure 3)" or "Table 2 compares…"
  normally stay plain. Bolding them would draw the eye away from the sentence,
  and most journals don't do it.

  So I'd make "Figure 1." and "Table 1." bold in the captions only. It's one
  line in the header (\captionsetup{labelfont=bf}), and it applies to every
  figure and table. Want me to add it?

✻ Baked for 23s · done 3:38 PM

❯ keep it. what about references before appendix?  what's the convention?       

⏺ Both are used. In most academic journals, and in APA and Chicago style, the
  references come before the appendices. The appendices are treated as extra
  material after the paper proper, which ends with its reference list.
  Statistics journals such as JASA follow this, and so do most social-science
  journals.

  The starter template you began from puts the appendices first and the
  references last. That's also common, especially in reports and theses, where
  the references close the whole document.

  Either is acceptable. Mainstream journal practice, though, is the paper, then
  references, then appendices. Vancouver numbering is unaffected: citations in
  the appendices keep their numbers, because the list is still ordered by first
  citation.

  In Quarto, it's a small change. An empty ::: {#refs} block marks where the
  list should go, so I'd put the References heading and that block after the
  Discussion, then the page break and the appendices. Switch to references
  before the appendices?

❯ do we need a conclusion after discussion?                                     

⏺ No, it isn't needed.

  - The instructions require only a title, author, date, abstract, introduction,
    data section and references. Nothing asks for a conclusion.
  - Your class notes put the summary inside the Discussion ("brief summary of
    the paper … key things we learned"), and the Discussion already opens with
    that paragraph. It ends on the main conclusion: the choice of beach says
    more about the water quality than the sign.
  - Length. A separate Conclusion would mostly repeat that paragraph and the
    abstract.

  There's one fair criticism of the current ending: the Discussion finishes on
  [Next steps], which is a quieter note than the main finding. Your own papers
  close with an "In summary, …" paragraph. If you'd like that shape, I'd move 
  the summary paragraph to the end of the Discussion, after Next steps, and open
  it with "In summary,". It wouldn't add any length. But the class notes
  suggest opening the Discussion with the summary, so keeping it where it is
  also follows the brief.

  My recommendation is to leave it as it is. Want me to move it anyway?

❯ no, I want to finish this thing.  The asterisk by the title for the GitHub    
  repo is strange. better place for it?                                         

⏺ The asterisk comes from Quarto's thanks: field, which puts a footnote on the
  title. It's standard in LaTeX papers, but a symbol on a question mark does
  look odd.

  The instructions only require a footnote with the repo link, which is what
  thanks provides. Two better places for it:

  1. On your name (recommended). "Sean Murphy*", with the same footnote at the
     bottom of page 1. Journals often attach code and data statements to the
     author, and the link stays on the first page, where the requirement expects
     it to be seen.
  2. At the end of the [Software] paragraph in the Data section: "…and the paper
     is written in Quarto [15].¹", with the footnote below. It sits naturally
     next to the software, but it's on page 3, so it's easy to miss.

  Shall I move it to your name?

✻ Brewed for 11s · done 3:49 PM

❯ Title: are some beaches safer than other?  is that better to put it in the    
  positive?                                                                     

⏺ I'd keep "Less Safe". The title's question should line up with the subtitle's
  answer, and your subtitle names the two worst beaches ("Marie Curtis Park East
  and Sunnyside exceed the swimming limit…"). "Are some beaches less safe?" is
  answered directly by it. "Are some beaches safer?" would lead the reader to
  expect the clean beaches to be named.

  "Less safe" also puts the weight on the risk, which is what the paper's advice
  is about: where swimmers should be careful. The positive version isn't wrong,
  but it pulls against the subtitle.

  If you do switch, note the plural: "Are Some Beaches Safer Than Others?".

✻ Baked for 6s · done 3:50 PM

❯ the title still seems awkward to me. give me five other variations or         
  suggestions                                                                   

⏺ Here are five options. Each follows the rubric: it's informative, tells the
  reader the story, and has no puns.

  1. Where You Swim Matters: E. coli at Toronto's Ten Beaches, 2007–2026
     The finding first, then what and when. The current subtitle still fits it.
  2. Two Toronto Beaches Exceed the E. coli Limit Far More Often Than the Rest
     A plain statement of the main result. The subtitle would then carry the
     numbers and the finding about the signs.
  3. E. coli at Toronto's Beaches: Which Exceed the Swimming Limit, and How Well
     Do the Daily Signs Work?
     Covers both questions the paper answers, in the "a question and an
     approach" format from your notes.
  4. Choosing a Beach Matters More Than Checking the Sign: Twenty Years of E. 
     coli at Toronto's Beaches
     The paper's conclusion as the title. It's the strongest claim, and it
     matches the abstract's last sentence.
  5. E. coli at Toronto's Beaches: Two Beaches Stand Apart, and Daily Signs Lag 
     the Water
     Both findings in short form, in the "content: specific content" pattern.

  My pick is 1. It's short, says what the paper is about, and states the main
  point without overclaiming. It also works with your current subtitle, which
  then gives the numbers. 4 is the strongest if you want the title to carry the
  whole message, but it's long.
